Transpension: The Right Adjoint to the Pi-type Thanks: Andreas Nuyts holds a Postdoctoral Fellowship from the Research Foundation - Flanders (FWO), and carried out most of this research holding a PhD Fellowship from the Research Foundation - Flanders (FWO). This research was partially conducted at Vrije Universiteit Brussel and funded by the Research Foundation - Flanders (FWO; G0G0519N). This research is partially funded by the Research Fund KU Leuven.
Andreas Nuyts*, Dominique Devriese ∘ \circ

Abstract
. Presheaf models of dependent type theory have been successfully applied to model HoTT, parametricity, and directed, guarded and nominal type theory. There has been considerable interest in internalizing aspects of these presheaf models, either to make the resulting language more expressive, or in order to carry out further reasoning internally, allowing greater abstraction and sometimes automated verification. While the constructions of presheaf models largely follow a common pattern, approaches towards internalization do not. Throughout the literature, various internal presheaf operators ( √ \mathbin{\surd} , Φ / 𝖾𝗑𝗍𝖾𝗇𝗍 \Phi/\mathsf{extent} , Ψ / 𝖦𝖾𝗅 \Psi/\mathsf{Gel} , 𝖦𝗅𝗎𝖾 \mathsf{Glue} , 𝖶𝖾𝗅𝖽 \mathsf{Weld} , 𝗆𝗂𝗅𝗅 \mathsf{mill} , the strictness axiom and locally fresh names) can be found and little is known about their relative expressiveness. Moreover, some of these require that variables whose type is a shape (representable presheaf, e.g. an interval) be used affinely. We propose a novel type former, the transpension type, which is right adjoint to universal quantification over a shape. Its structure resembles a dependent version of the suspension type in HoTT. We give general typing rules and a presheaf semantics in terms of base category functors dubbed multipliers. Structural rules for shape variables and certain aspects of the transpension type depend on characteristics of the multiplier. We demonstrate how the transpension type and the strictness axiom can be combined to implement all and improve some of the aforementioned internalization operators (without formal claim in the case of locally fresh names).



1.
Introduction and Related Work

1.1.
The power of presheaves

Presheaf semantics
[
Hof97
,
HS97
]
are an excellent tool for modelling relational preservation properties of (dependent) type theory. They have been applied to
parametricity (which is about preservation of relations)
[
AGJ14
,
BCM15
,
ND18a
,
NVD17
]
,
univalent type theory (preservation of equivalences)
[
BCH14
,
CMS20
,
CCHM17
,
Hub16
,
KL18
,
Ort18
,
OP18
]
,
directed type theory (preservation of morphisms),
guarded type theory (preservation of the stage of advancement of computation)
[
BM20
]

and even combinations thereof
[
BBC+19
,
CH21
,
RS17
,
WL20
]
.
1
1

1

We omit models that are not explicitly structured as presheaf models
[
AHH18
,
LH11
,
Nor19
]
.

The presheaf models just cited almost all follow a common pattern: First one chooses a suitable base category
{\mathcal{W}}
.
The presheaf category over
{\mathcal{W}}
is automatically a model of dependent type theory with the important basic type formers
[
Hof97
]
as well as a tower of universes
[
HS97
]
.
Next, one identifies a suitable notion of fibrancy and replaces or supplements the existing type judgement
\Gamma\vdash T\,\mathsf{type}
with one that classifies fibrant types:

HoTT:

For homotopy type theory (HoTT,
[
Uni13
]
), one considers Kan fibrant types, i.e. presheaves in which edges can be composed and inverted as in an
\infty
-groupoid.
The precise definition may differ in different treatments.

Parametricity:

For parametric type theory, one considers discrete types
[
AGJ14
,
CH21
,
ND18a
,
NVD17
]
: essentially those that satisfy Reynolds’ identity extension property
[
Rey83
]
which states that homogeneously related objects are equal. This can be expressed by requiring that any non-dependent function
{\mathbb{I}}\to A
from the relational interval, is constant.

Directed:

In directed type theory, one may want to consider Segal, covariant, discrete and Rezk types
[
RS17
]
and possibly also Conduché types
[
Gir64
,
Nuy18b
]
[
Nuy20
, ex. 8.1.27]
.

Guarded:

In guarded type theory, one considers clock-irrelevant types
[
BM20
]
: types
A
such that any non-dependent function
{\text{\clock}}\to A
from the clock type, is constant.

Nominal:

Nominal type theory
[
Che12
,
PMD15
]
can be modelled in the Schanuel topos
[
Pit13b
, §6.3]
.
This is the subcategory of nullary affine cubical sets (see
example

6.16
later on) that send pushouts in the base category to pullbacks in
{\mathsf{Set}}
.
This ensures that if a cell depending on names
\mathopen{}\left\{i,j,k\right\}\mathclose{}
in fact only depends on
\mathopen{}\left\{i,j\right\}\mathclose{}
and in fact also only depends on
\mathopen{}\left\{i,k\right\}\mathclose{}
, then it only depends on
\mathopen{}\left\{i\right\}\mathclose{}
.

To the extent possible, one subsequently proves that the relevant notions of fibrancy are closed under basic type formers, so that we can restrict to fibrant types and still carry out most of the familiar type-theoretic reasoning and programming.
Special care is required for the universe
\mathsf{U}
: it is generally straightforward to adapt the standard Hofmann-Streicher universe to classify only fibrant types, but the universe of fibrant types is in general not automatically fibrant itself.

HoTT:

In HoTT, the Hofmann-Streicher universe of Kan types is usually automatically Kan.

Parametricity:

In earlier work on parametricity with Vezzosi
[
NVD17
,
ND18a
]
, we made the universe of discrete types discrete by modifying its presheaf structure and introduced a parametric modality in order to use that universe.
In contrast, Atkey et al.
[
AGJ14
]
and Cavallo and Harper
[
CH21
]
simply accept that their universes of discrete types are not discrete.

Directed:

In directed type theory, one could expect, perhaps via a directed univalence result
[
WL20
]
, that the universe of covariant types is Segal.

Guarded:

In guarded type theory, Bizjak et al.
[
BGC+16
]
let the universe depend on a collection of in-scope clock variables lest the clock-indexed later modality
\rhd:\forall(\kappa:{\text{\clock}}).\mathsf{U}_{\Delta}\to\mathsf{U}_{\Delta}
(where
\kappa\in\Delta
) be non-dependent and therefore constant (not clock-indexed) by clock-irrelevance of
\mathsf{U}\to\mathsf{U}

[
BM20
]
.

1.2.
Internalizing the power of presheaves

Purely metatheoretic results about type theory certainly have their value.
Parametricity, for instance, has originated and proven its value as a metatheoretic technique for reasoning about programs.
However, with dependent type theory being not only a programming language but also a logic, it is preferable to formulate results about it within the type system, rather than outside it.
We highlight two particular motivations for doing so: to enlarge the end user’s toolbox, and to be able to prove internally that a type is fibrant.

Enlarging the end user’s toolbox

One motivation for internalizing metatheorems is to enlarge the toolbox of the end user of the proof assistant.
If this is the only goal, then we can prove the desired results in the model on pen and paper and then internalize them ad hoc with an axiom with or without computation rules.

HoTT:

Book HoTT
[
Uni13
]
simply postulates the univalence axiom without computational behaviour, as justified e.g. by the model of Kan-fibrant simplicial sets
[
KL18
]
.

CCHM cubical type theory
[
CCHM17
]
provides the
\mathsf{Glue}
type, which comes with introduction, elimination,
\beta
- and
\eta
-rules and which turns the univalence axiom into a theorem with computational behaviour. It also contains CCHM-Kan-fibrancy of all types as an axiom, in the form of the CCHM-Kan composition operator, with decreed computational behaviour that is defined by induction on the type.

Parametricity:

Bernardy, Coquand and Moulin
[
BCM15
,
Mou16
]
(henceforth: BCM) internalize their (unary, but generalizable to
k
-ary) cubical set model of parametricity using two combinators
\Phi
and
\Psi

[
Mou16
]
, a.k.a.
\mathsf{extent}
and
\mathsf{Gel}

[
CH21
]
.
\Phi
internalizes the presheaf structure of the function type, and
\Psi
that of the universe.

The combinator
\Phi
and at first sight also
\Psi
require that the cubical set model lacks diagonals.
Indeed, to construct a value over the primitive interval,
\Phi
and
\Psi
each take one argument for every endpoint and one argument for the edge as a whole.
Nested use of these combinators, e.g. to create a square, will take
(k+1)^{2}
arguments for
k^{2}
vertices,
2k
sides and
1
square as a whole but none for specifying the diagonal.
For this reason, BCM’s type system enforces a form of
affine
use of interval variables.
Similarly, connections as in CCHM
[
CCHM17
]
are ruled out.
In the current paper, we will see that these requirements are not absolute for
\Psi
: there is apparently a very natural ‘automatic’ way to define the behaviour on diagonals and connections where the
\Psi
-type is not explicitly specified by its arguments.

In earlier work with Vezzosi
[
NVD17
]
, we have internalized parametricity instead using the
\mathsf{Glue}
type
[
CCHM17
]
and its dual
\mathsf{Weld}
.
Later on, we added a primitive
\mathsf{mill}

[
ND18b
]
for swapping
\mathsf{Weld}
and
\Pi(i:{\mathbb{I}})
.
These operations are sound in presheaves over any base category where we can multiply with
{\mathbb{I}}
– including cube categories with diagonals or connections – and are (therefore) strictly less expressive than
\Phi
which is not.
Discreteness of all types was internalized as a non-computing
path degeneracy
axiom.
2
2

2

It is worth noting that it was not possible to use affine interval variables in the setting of
[
NVD17
]
: The type system features parametric
\Pi
-types which are modelled as ordinary
\Pi
-types with non-discrete domain. Discreteness of the
\Pi
-type can be proven solely from discreteness of the codomain, simply by swapping interval variable and function argument. This is however not possible in the affine setting, where only variables introduced prior to an interval variable are taken to be fresh for that interval variable and the exchange rule with an interval variable only works one way.

Directed:

Weaver and Licata
[
WL20
]
use a bicubical set model to show that directed HoTT
[
RS17
]
can be soundly extended with a directed univalence
axiom
.

Guarded:

In guarded type theory
[
BM20
]
,
one axiomatizes Löb induction and clock-irrelevance.

Nominal:

One version of nominal type theory
[
PMD15
]
provides the locally fresh name abstraction
\nu(i:{\mathbb{I}})
which can be used anywhere (i.e. the goal type remains the same after we abstract over a fresh name). The operation introduces a name but requires a body that is fresh for the name (i.e. we do not get to use it). This would be rather useless, were it not that we are allowed to
capture
the fresh name (see
section

10
).

Internalizing fibrancy proofs

Another motivation to internalize aspects of presheaf categories, is for building parts of the model inside the type theory, thus abstracting away certain categorical details such as the very definition of presheaves, and for some type systems enabling automatic verification of these constructions.
Given the common pattern in models described in the previous section, it is particularly attractive to try and define fibrancy and prove results about it internally.

In the context of HoTT, Orton and Pitts
[
Ort18
,
OP18
]
study CCHM-Kan-fibrancy
[
CCHM17
]
in a type theory extended with a set of axioms, of which all but one serve to characterize the interval and the notion of cofibration. One axiom,
strictness
, provides a type former
\mathsf{Strict}
for strictifying partial isomorphisms, which exists in every presheaf category.
In order to construct a universe of fibrant types, Licata et al. postulate an “amazing right adjoint”
{\mathbb{I}}\mathbin{\surd}{\llcorner\hskip-1.72218pt\lrcorner}
to the non-dependent path functor
{\mathbb{I}}\to{\llcorner\hskip-1.72218pt\lrcorner}

[
LOPS18
,
Ort18
]
, which indeed exists in presheaves over cartesian base categories if
{\mathbb{I}}
is representable.
Since
{\mathbb{I}}\mathbin{\surd}{\llcorner\hskip-1.72218pt\lrcorner}
and its related axioms are global operations (only applicable to closed terms, unless you want to open Pandora’s box as we do in the current paper), they keep everything sound by introducing a judgemental comonadic
global
modality
\flat
.

Orton et al.’s formalization
[
LOPS18
,
Ort18
,
OP18
]
is only what we call
meta-internal
: the argument is internalized to
some
type theory which still only serves as a metatheory of the type system of interest.
Ideally, we would also be able to define and prove fibrancy of types
within
the type theory of interest, which we call
auto-internal
. This has several advantages:

•

A general approach to auto-internalization of notions of fibrancy saves us from a proliferation of type systems, each with axiomatic internal fibrancy operations with hard-coded computational behaviour that proceeds by case analysis on the construction of the type. Proving fibrancy auto-internally will in general be more typesafe than hard-coding it in a language implementation that is often written in a simply-typed language such as Haskell and OCaml.

•

Given an auto-internal implementation, we can still pretend that we have a meta-internal situation by restricting ourselves to a subset of the language. But we automatically get a two-level type theory
[
Voe13
,
ACK17
]
, where we have access to non-fibrant types from within. (This does not prove conservativity of two-level type theory over the object system.)

•

In directed type theory, there are various relevant notions of fibrancy, many of which are not well preserved by basic type formers, so access to non-fibrant types may be a necessity to get any work done at all.

Auto-internal treatments exist of discrete types in parametricity
[
CH21
]
, and discrete, fibrewise-Segal and Rezk types in directed type theory
[
RS17
]
, but not yet for covariant, Segal or Kan fibrant types due to the need to consider paths in the context
{\mathbb{I}}\to\Gamma
.

1.3.
The transpension type

What is striking about the previous section is that, while most authors have been able to solve their own problems, a common approach is completely absent. We have encountered
\Phi
and
\Psi

[
Mou16
]
, the amazing right adjoint
\mathbin{\surd}

[
LOPS18
]
,
\mathsf{Glue}

[
CCHM17
,
NVD17
]
,
\mathsf{Weld}

[
NVD17
]
,
\mathsf{mill}

[
ND18b
]
, the strictness axiom
[
OP18
]
and locally fresh names
[
PMD15
]
. We have also seen that
\Phi
and
\Psi
presently require an affine base category, and that
\mathbin{\surd}
presently requires the global modality
\flat
.

The goal of the current paper is to develop a smaller collection of internal primitives that impose few restrictions on the choice of base category and allow the internal construction of the aforementioned operators when sound.
To this end, we introduce the
transpension
type former
{\between}[{i}]:\mathrm{Ty}(\Gamma)\to\mathrm{Ty}(\Gamma,i:{\mathbb{I}})
which in cartesian settings is right adjoint to
\Pi(i:{\mathbb{I}}):\mathrm{Ty}(\Gamma,i:{\mathbb{I}})\to\mathrm{Ty}(\Gamma)
and is therefore not a quantifier binding
i
, but a coquantifier that
depends
on it.
This same operation was already considered in topoi by Yetter
[
Yet87
]
, who named it
\nabla
.
Using the transpension and
\mathsf{Strict}
, we can construct
\Phi
(when sound),
\Psi
,
\mathbin{\surd}
and
\mathsf{Glue}
, and heuristically translate a subsystem of the nominal dependent type system FreshMLTT
[
PMD15
]
featuring variable capture and locally fresh names.
Given a type former for certain pushouts, we can also construct
\mathsf{Weld}
and
\mathsf{mill}
.

The transpension coquantifier
{\between}[{u:{\mathbb{U}}}]:\mathrm{Ty}(\Gamma)\to\mathrm{Ty}(\Gamma,u:{\mathbb{U}})
is part of a sequence of adjoints
\Sigma\,{u}\dashv\Omega[{u}]\dashv\Pi\,{u}\dashv{\between}[{u}]
, preceded by the
\Sigma
-type, weakening and the
\Pi
-type.
Adjointness of the first three is provable from the structural rules of type theory.
However, it is not immediately clear how to add typing rules for a further adjoint.
Birkedal et al.
[
BCM+20
]
explain how to add a single modality that has a left adjoint in the semantics.
If we want to have two or more adjoint modalities internally, then we can use a multimodal type system such as MTT
[
GKNB21
,
GKNB20a
]
.
Each modality in MTT needs a semantic left adjoint, so we can only internalize
\Omega[{u}]
,
\Pi\,{u}
and
{\between}[{u}]
.
A drawback which we accept (as a challenge for future work), is that
\Omega[{u}]
and
\Pi\,{u}
become modalities which are a bit more awkward to deal with than ordinary weakening and
\Pi
-types.

A further complication is that the aforementioned modalities bind or depend on a variable, a phenomenon which is not supported by MTT.
We solve this by grouping shape variables such as
u:{\mathbb{U}}
in a
shape context
which is not considered part of the type-theoretic context but instead serves as the
mode
of the judgement.
This way, we are also rid of the requirement that all internal operations commute with shape substitution; in fact, the transpension generally does not (
section

2.1.7
,
[
Nuy23b
]
).

1.4.
Contributions

Our central contribution is to reduce the plethora of interal presheaf operators in the literature to only a few operations.

•

To this end, we formulate a type system MTraS featuring a
transpension type

{\between}[{u:{\mathbb{U}}}]
, right adjoint to
\Pi({u:{\mathbb{U}}})
, with typing rules built on
extensional
MTT
[
GKNB21
,
GKNB20a
]
.
We explain how it is reminiscent of the suspension type from HoTT
[
Uni13
]
.

•

More generally, the transpension type can be right adjoint to any quantifier-like operation
\forall({u:{\mathbb{U}}})
which need neither respect the exchange rule, nor weakening or contraction. In this setting, we also introduce the
fresh weakening
coquantifier
\Finv[{u:{\mathbb{U}}}]
, which is left adjoint to
\forall({u:{\mathbb{U}}})
and therefore coincides with weakening
\Omega[{u:{\mathbb{U}}}]
in cartesian settings.

•

We provide a categorical semantics for
{\between}[{u:{\mathbb{U}}}]
in almost any presheaf category
\mathrm{Psh}({\mathcal{W}})
over base category
{\mathcal{W}}
, for almost any representable object
{\mathbb{U}}=\mathbf{y}U
,
U\in{\mathcal{W}}
.
To accommodate non-cartesian variables, our system is not parametrized by a representable object
{\mathbb{U}}=\mathbf{y}U
, but by an arbitrary endofunctor
{\llcorner\hskip-1.72218pt\lrcorner}\ltimes U
on
{\mathcal{W}}
: the
multiplier
.
3
3

3

In the technical report
[
Nuy23b
]
, we generalize multipliers beyond
endo
functors.

We introduce
criteria
for characterizing the multiplier
(
definition

6.2
)
which we use as requirements for internal type theoretic features.
We identify a complication for base categories (most notably in guarded and nominal type theory) that are not
objectwise pointable
, and define dimensionally split morphisms (a generalization of split epimorphisms) in order to include those base categories.
We exhibit relevant multipliers in base categories found in the literature (
section

6.3
).

•

We show that
all general presheaf internalization operators
that we are aware of – viz.
\Phi
/
\mathsf{extent}

(when sound),

\Psi
/
\mathsf{Gel}

[
Mou16
,
BCM15
]
, the amazing right adjoint
\mathbin{\surd}

[
LOPS18
]
,
\mathsf{Glue}

[
CCHM17
,
NVD17
]
,
\mathsf{Weld}

[
NVD17
]
,
\mathsf{mill}

[
ND18b
]
and (with no formal claim) locally fresh names – can be
recovered
from just the transpension type, the strictness axiom and pushouts along
\mathsf{snd}:\varphi\times A\to A
where
\varphi:\mathsf{Prop}
(see
fig.

9
for a dependency graph).
In the process, some of these operators can be
improved
: We generalize
\Psi
from affine-like (
\top
-slice full) to arbitrary multipliers, including cartesian ones and we justify
{\mathbb{U}}\mathbin{\surd}{\llcorner\hskip-1.72218pt\lrcorner}
without a global modality and get
\beta
- and
\eta
-rules for it.
Moreover, since our system provides an operation
\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}{\between}[{u}]}}
for quantifying over contexts, we take a step towards auto-internalizing Orton et al.’s work
[
LOPS18
,
Ort18
,
OP18
]
.
When
\Phi
is not sound (e.g. in settings with diagonals or connections), we suggest the internal notion of
transpensive
types to retain some of its power.
Finally, a form of higher dimensional pattern matching is enabled by exposing
\forall({u:{\mathbb{U}}})
internally as a left adjoint.

•

In a technical report
[
Nuy23b
]
, we investigate how the modalities introduced in this paper commute with each other, and with prior modalities (i.e. those already present before adding the transpension type). We also consider composite multipliers, and natural transformations between modalities (called 2-cells in MTT) arising from natural transformations between multipliers.

While MTT
[
GKNB21
,
GKNB20a
]
satisfies canonicity and even normalization insofar as its modality system (called the
mode theory
) does
[
Gra22
]
,
we will instantiate MTT on a mode theory for which we presently do not have a computational theory,
and we will extend it with some additional typing rules.
For this reason, we build on extensional MTT
[
GKNB20a
]
, and defer canonicity and decidability of type-checking to future work.

1.5.
Overview of the paper

In
section

2
, we study in a simplified setting (a system called FFTraS) how the transpension type resembles the suspension type from HoTT and demonstrate how to put it in action.
In
section

3
, we give a brief overview of the typing rules of MTT and decorate the MTT syntax with
left adjoint reminders
.
In
section

4
, we define the mode theory of MTraS, an instantiation of MTT. This mode theory is extremely general and essentially contains all semantic adjoint pairs of a given domain and codomain as modalities between the corresponding modes. In the next two sections, we highlight a number of interesting modalities:
in
section

5
we consider modalities related to shape substitutions, and in
section

6
we define and study multipliers and consider modalities arising from them, including the transpension modality.
In
section

7
, we pseudo-embed FFTraS in MTraS and generalize some of the results obtained for FFTraS.
In
section

8
, we supplement MTraS with a few specialized typing rules.
In
section

9
, we investigate the structure of the transpension type in MTraS.
In
section

10
, we explain how to recover known internal presheaf operators.
We conclude in
section

11
.

2.
First Steps: A Fully Faithful Transpension System (FFTraS)

In this section, for purposes of demonstration, we present simplified typing rules for the transpension type which apply in a specific setting (as proven in
section

7
).
Using these, we will already be able to exhibit the transpension type as similar to a dependent version of the suspension type in HoTT
[
Uni13
]
, and to prove internally that it is right adjoint to universal quantification.
Moreover, in order to showcase how the transpension type allows us to internalize the presheaf structure of other types, we will demonstrate a technique which we call higher-dimensional pattern matching and which has already been demonstrated by Pitts
[
Pit14
]
in nominal type theory using locally fresh names
[
PMD15
]
.

Linear/affine shape variables:

\dfrac{\begin{array}[]{l l}\hskip-3.87498pt\text{\hypertarget{rule:ff:ctx-shp}{{ff:ctx-shp}}}\\
\Gamma\,\mathsf{ctx}\end{array}\hfill}{\begin{array}[]{l l}\Gamma,u:{\mathbb{U}}\,\mathsf{ctx}\end{array}\hfill}\text{\text{}}\hskip 18.49988pt\dfrac{\begin{array}[]{l l}\hskip-3.87498pt\text{\hypertarget{rule:ff:ctx-shp:fmap}{{ff:ctx-shp:fmap}}}\\
\sigma:\Gamma\to\Gamma^{\prime}\end{array}\hfill}{\begin{array}[]{l l}(\sigma,u/u^{\prime}):(\Gamma,u:{\mathbb{U}})\to(\Gamma^{\prime},u^{\prime}:{\mathbb{U}})\end{array}\hfill}\text{\text{}}\hskip 18.49988pt\dfrac{\begin{array}[]{l l}\hskip-3.87498pt\text{\hypertarget{rule:ff:ctx-shp:wkn}{{ff:ctx-shp:wkn}} (optional)}\\
\sigma:\Gamma\to\Gamma^{\prime}\end{array}\hfill}{\begin{array}[]{l l}\sigma:(\Gamma,u:{\mathbb{U}})\to\Gamma^{\prime}\end{array}\hfill}\text{}

Linear/affine function type:

\dfrac{\begin{array}[]{l l}\hskip-3.87498pt\text{\hypertarget{rule:ff:forall}{{ff:forall}}}\\
\Gamma,u:{\mathbb{U}}\vdash A\,\mathsf{type}\end{array}\hfill}{\begin{array}[]{l l}\Gamma\vdash\forall\,{u}.A\,\mathsf{type}\end{array}\hfill}\text{\text{}}\hskip 18.49988pt\dfrac{\begin{array}[]{l l}\hskip-3.87498pt\text{\hypertarget{rule:ff:forall:intro}{{ff:forall:intro}}}\\
\Gamma,u:{\mathbb{U}}\vdash a:A\end{array}\hfill}{\begin{array}[]{l l}\Gamma\vdash\lambda u.a:\forall\,{u}.A\end{array}\hfill}\text{\text{}}\hskip 18.49988pt\dfrac{\begin{array}[]{l l}\hskip-3.87498pt\text{\hypertarget{rule:ff:forall:elim}{{ff:forall:elim}}}\\
\Gamma\vdash f:\forall\,{u}.A\\
\text{No shape vars in $\Delta$}\end{array}\hfill}{\begin{array}[]{l l}\Gamma,u:{\mathbb{U}},\delta:\Delta\vdash f\,u:A\end{array}\hfill}\text{\text{}}

Telescope quantification:

\dfrac{\begin{array}[]{l l}\hskip-3.87498pt\text{\hypertarget{rule:ff:ctx-forall}{{ff:ctx-forall}}}\\
\Gamma,u:{\mathbb{U}},\delta:\Delta\,\mathsf{ctx}\\

Transpension type:

\dfrac{\begin{array}[]{l l}\hskip-3.87498pt\text{\hypertarget{rule:ff:transp}{{ff:transp}}}\\

Figure 1.
Selection of typing rules for a fully faithful transpension type.

2.1.
Typing rules

To do this, we first present, in
fig.

1
, typing rules for the transpension type in a specific setting:
a dependent type system with linear or affine shape variables
u:{\mathbb{U}}
,
where shape variable contraction is forbidden.

2.1.1.
Linear/affine shape variables

Variables to the left of
u
are understood to be fresh for
u
; variables introduced after
u
may be substituted with terms depending on
u
.
In particular, we have no contraction
(w/u,w/v):(w:{\mathbb{U}})\to(u,v:{\mathbb{U}})
, while exchange
(x:A,u:{\mathbb{U}})\to(u:{\mathbb{U}},x:A)
only works in one direction.
This is enforced by the special substitution rule for shape variables (
).
Weakening of shape variables is optionally allowed (
).
The examples in which the type system will be put to use, are agnostic as to whether exchange of shape variables
(u:{\mathbb{U}},v:{\mathbb{U}})\to(v:{\mathbb{U}},u:{\mathbb{U}})
is possible and models of both situations exist.

2.1.2.
Linear/affine function type

The system features a linear/affine function type
\forall\,{u}.A
over
{\mathbb{U}}
, with unsurprising formation and introduction rules (
,
).
The rule for
f\,u
(
) requires that the function
f
be fresh for
u
, i.e. that
f
depend only on variables to the left of
u

[
BCM15
,
Mou16
]
. For simplicity, we require that
u
is the last shape variable in the context.

2.1.3.
Transpension type

Additionally, the system contains a transpension type
{\between}[{u}]\,A
over
{\mathbb{U}}
, with more unusual rules.
Similar to the
introduction rule
of multimode type theory (MTT) (
in
fig.

4
), the
meridian
constructor of the transpension type (
) works by dependent transposition
[
BCM+20
]
[
Nuy18a
, §2.1.3]
[
Nuy20
, §5.1.3-5.2]
: a term of type
{\between}[{u}]\,A
in a given context, is equivalent to a term of type
A
in the context obtained by applying the left adjoint to
{\between}[{u}]
– which is universal quantification over
u
– to the context.
However, the situation is a bit more subtle than in MTT, in the sense that the left adjoint
\forall\,{u}
is itself a binder and therefore acts on objects already living in a context. For this reason, we consider the entire situation in a further context
\Gamma
. So we start from a context
\Gamma
, a telescope
\delta:\Delta
(where
\delta
denotes the vector of variables in the telescope) in context
\Gamma,u:{\mathbb{U}}
and a term
a:A
that does not live in telescope
\Delta
but in the universally quantified telescope
\forall\,{u}.(\delta:\Delta)

[
BV17
,
ND19
]
, which extends
\Gamma
.
The resulting meridian
\mathsf{mer}[{u}]\,a:{\between}[{u}]\,A
then lives in telescope
\Delta
, which extends
\Gamma,u:{\mathbb{U}}
.
Thus, we remark that both
{\between}[{u}]\,A
and
\mathsf{mer}[{u}]\,a
depend on
u
, whereas
A
and
a
do not, so in a way the transpension lifts data to a higher dimension, turning points into
{\mathbb{U}}
-cells.

The type formation rule
is parallel to the modal type formation rule of MTT (
in
fig.

4
), which internalizes a (weak) dependent right adjoint; its premises are such that the introduction rule is well-typed.

The elimination rule
is equivalent to the existence of the function

\lambda f.\mathsf{unmer}(u.f\,u):(\forall\,{u}.{\between}[{u}]\,A)\to A,

which is essentially the co-unit of the adjunction; this differs from the elimination rule of MTT (
in
fig.

4
) which works by pattern-matching, but is parallel to the projection function in
proposition

3.3
, as are the
\beta
- and
\eta
-rules which internalize the adjunction laws and to which we get back in
section

2.1.6
.
The elimination rule takes data again to a lower dimension: it turns a dependent
{\mathbb{U}}
-cell in the transpension into a point in
A
.

2.1.4.
Admissibility of telescope rules

The typing rules for the transpension type rely on the unusual notion of
telescope quantification and application
, which remain to be discussed.
Before doing so, we remark that one can take either of two viewpoints w.r.t. these rules.
One can take a syntactic viewpoint, viewing each of the typing rules concerned as a formal typing rule, i.e. as a constructor of our generalized algebraic syntax
[
Car86
,
Car78
,
AK16
]
.
Alternatively, it is possible to prove metatheoretically that each of these rules is admissible, by defining
\forall\,{u}.(\delta:\Delta)
as a telescope of the same length as
\Delta
, but where each variable’s type is universally quantified over
u:{\mathbb{U}}
.
This latter view is the one that inspires most of our notations, but we make a point of not violating the former possibility, because that one allows a pseudo-embedding
4
4

4

Pseudo, because
is only an isomorphism in the general system, but that would undidactically complicate notations in the current section.
of the current specialized system in the main system of this paper (
section

7
).

2.1.5.
Telescope quantification

Given a context
\Gamma,u:{\mathbb{U}},\delta:\Delta
with no shape variables in
\Delta
, the rule
creates a new context
\Gamma,\forall\,{u}.(\delta:\Delta)
, which is just
\Gamma
again if
\Delta
has zero variables (
).
From the syntactic viewpoint, it would perhaps be cleaner to write something like
[\forall\,{u}](\Gamma,u:{\mathbb{U}},\delta:\Delta)
, or more generally
[\forall\,{u}]\Theta
for any context
\Theta
featuring
u:{\mathbb{U}}
as its last shape variable.
However, the notation we have chosen is
possible
since every such context is of the form
\Theta=\Gamma,u:{\mathbb{U}},\delta:\Delta
for some context
\Gamma
and telescope
\Delta
, and moreover it is justified by the admissibility proof as well as in the following sense:

(1)

The variables in
\Gamma
can be accessed in context
[\forall\,{u}](\Gamma,u:{\mathbb{U}},\delta:\Delta)
,

(2)

For every variable
y:B
in
\Delta
, we get a term of type
\forall\,{v}.B[\sigma]
in context
[\forall\,{u}](\Gamma,u:{\mathbb{U}},\delta:\Delta)
(for a suitable substitution
\sigma
).

To see (1), we make use of the functoriality rule
,
which from the syntactic viewpoint we could more cleanly write as
[\forall({u/u^{\prime}})]\rho:[\forall\,{u}]\Theta\to[\forall\,{u^{\prime}}]\Theta^{\prime}
for
\rho:\Theta\to\Theta^{\prime}
.
The alternative notation in the typing rule is again
possible
since any such
\rho
is of the form
\rho=(\sigma,u/u^{\prime},\tau/\delta^{\prime})
for some
\sigma:\Gamma\to\Gamma^{\prime}
and well-typed vector of terms
\tau
, and justified for similar reasons as above.
By applying functoriality to the weakening substitution
(\Gamma,u:{\mathbb{U}},\delta:\Delta)\to(\Gamma,u:{\mathbb{U}})
, we get a substitution
(\Gamma,\forall\,{u}.(\delta:\Delta))\to(\Gamma,\forall\,{u}.())=\Gamma
, which we can use to ignore
\forall\,{u}.(\delta:\Delta)
altogether and thus get access to the variables in context
\Gamma
.

To see (2), we need telescope application.

The transpension type and the meridian constructor respect substitution (
,
), and this can only be stated thanks to functoriality (
).

2.1.6.
Telescope application

In
section

2.1.3
above, we noted that the formation and introduction rules of the transpension type are in line with those of the modal type in MTT (
fig.

4
) and act by dependent transposition.
In fact, the same is true for the formation and introduction rules of the linear/affine function type, which is a dependent right adjoint to shape variable extension of contexts (
).
In order for the types to be adjoints internally – which requires that we can define unit and co-unit functions and that the adjunction laws are statable and satisfied – we need their left adjoint operations to be adjoints, i.e. for any context
\Psi
and any context
\Theta=(\Gamma,u:{\mathbb{U}},\Delta)
, we need substitutions
\Psi\to[\forall\,{u}]\Theta=(\Gamma,\forall\,{u}.(\delta:\Delta))
to be equivalent to substitutions
(\Psi,u:{\mathbb{U}})\to\Theta=(\Gamma,u:{\mathbb{U}},\delta:\Delta)
respecting
u
.

One way to ensure this is by providing natural unit and co-unit substitutions.
For the unit, we need substitutions
\Psi\to[\forall\,{u}](\Psi,u:{\mathbb{U}})=(\Psi,\forall\,{u}.())=\Psi
, so we can take the identity.
In other words, the unit is given by
, with naturality given by
.

For the co-unit, we need substitutions
(\Gamma,\forall\,{u}.(\delta:\Delta),v:{\mathbb{U}})\to(\Gamma,u:{\mathbb{U}},\Delta)
, which are given by
and made natural by
.
Again, from the syntactic viewpoint it would be cleaner to write
\mathsf{app}_{\Theta}:([\forall\,{u}]\Theta,v:{\mathbb{U}})\to\Theta
.
However, intuitively, semantically and in the admissibility proof, what it does is applying the ‘function’
{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}u.\delta:\forall\,{u}.\Delta
to
v:{\mathbb{U}}
, which inspires the notation in the typing rule.

Since the unit is the identity, the adjunction laws simply require that whiskering the co-unit with either adjoint also yields the identity.
The fact that
\mathsf{app}_{(\Gamma,u:{\mathbb{U}})}=1_{(\Gamma,u:{\mathbb{U}})}
is exactly what is asserted by
.
The fact that
[\forall({v/u})]\mathsf{app}_{(\Gamma,u:{\mathbb{U}},\delta:\Delta)}=1_{(\Gamma,\forall\,{u}.(\delta:\Delta))}
is exactly what is asserted by
.

With the unit and co-unit for the adjunction
(-,u:{\mathbb{U}})\dashv[\forall\,{u}]
on contexts in place, we can now state the
\beta
- and
\eta
-rules of the transpension type (
,
) parallel to those for the modal type in MTT (
proposition

3.3
).

We now show (2) from above, i.e. assuming
\Delta
lists a variable
y:B
, we seek to derive a term
\Gamma,\forall\,{u}.(\delta:\Delta)\vdash t:\forall\,{v}.B[\sigma]
. This can be done using
as follows:

\dfrac{\begin{array}[]{l l}\dfrac{\begin{array}[]{l l}\Gamma,u:{\mathbb{U}},\delta:\Delta\vdash y:B\end{array}\hfill}{\begin{array}[]{l l}\Gamma,\forall\,{u}.(\delta:\Delta),v:{\mathbb{U}}\vdash y[v/u,({\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}u.\delta)\,v/\delta]:B[v/u,({\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}u.\delta)\,v/\delta]\end{array}\hfill}\text{}\end{array}\hfill}{\begin{array}[]{l l}\Gamma,\forall\,{u}.(\delta:\Delta)\vdash\lambda v.(y[v/u,({\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}u.\delta)\,v/\delta]):\forall\,{v}.(B[v/u,({\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}u.\delta)\,v/\delta])\end{array}\hfill}\text{}.

Definition 2.1
.

For any variable
y:B
in telescope
\Delta
, we define
\Gamma,\forall\,{u}.(\delta:\Delta)\vdash{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}u.y:=\lambda v.(y[v/u,({\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}u.\delta)\,v/\delta]):\forall\,{v}.(B[v/u,({\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}u.\delta)\,v/\delta])
.

Proposition 2.2
.

For any variable
y:B
in telescope
\Delta

and any substitution
(1_{\Gamma},u/u,\tau/\delta):(\Gamma,u:{\mathbb{U}},\delta^{\prime}:\Delta^{\prime})\to(\Gamma,u:{\mathbb{U}},\delta:\Delta)
, we have
\Gamma\vdash({\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}u.y)[{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}u.\tau/{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}u.\delta]=\lambda u.(\tau_{y}[u/u,({\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}u.\delta^{\prime})\,u/\delta^{\prime}]):\forall\,{u}.B[\tau/\delta]
, where
\tau_{y}=y[\tau/\delta]
is the component of the vector
\tau
for variable
y
.

Proof 2.3
.

We have

\displaystyle({\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}u.y)[u/u,\tau/\delta]

\displaystyle=(\lambda u.y[u/u,({\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}u.\delta)\,u/\delta])[{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}u.\tau/{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}u.\delta]

(
definition

2.1
)

\displaystyle=\lambda u.(y[u/u,({\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}u.\delta)\,u/\delta][{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}u.\tau/{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}u.\delta,u/u])

\displaystyle=\lambda u.(y[u/u,\tau/\delta][u/u,({\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}u.\delta^{\prime})\,u/\delta^{\prime}])

(
)

\displaystyle=\lambda u.(\tau_{y}[u/u,({\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}u.\delta^{\prime})\,u/\delta^{\prime}]).

Corollary 2.4
.

For any variable
y:B
in telescope
\Delta

and any substitution
(1_{\Gamma},u/u,\tau/\delta):(\Gamma,u:{\mathbb{U}})\to(\Gamma,u:{\mathbb{U}},\delta:\Delta)
, we have
\Gamma\vdash({\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}u.y)[{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}u.\tau/{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}u.\delta]=\lambda u.\tau_{y}:\forall\,{u}.B[\tau/\delta]
, where
\tau_{y}=y[\tau/\delta]
is the component of the vector
\tau
for variable
y
.

Proof 2.5
.

This follows from
.

2.1.7.
Discussion

The type system presented above is less general than the paper’s main system MTraS.
In
section

2.1.6
, we saw that the unit of the adjunction on contexts is invertible.
This is equivalent to the left adjoint
(-,u:{\mathbb{U}}):\mathrm{Ctx}\to\mathrm{Ctx}/(u:{\mathbb{U}})
being fully faithful
[
nLa23a
]
, and the requirement on presheaf models to support the typing rules in the current section (with
an isomorphism) is exactly that: the
multiplier
functor interpreting
(-,u:{\mathbb{U}})
has to be fully faithful w.r.t. the slice category over
(u:{\mathbb{U}})
.

By uniqueness of the adjoint, we can also conclude that the co-unit of the adjunction
\forall\,{u}\dashv{\between}[{u}]
is invertible,
5
5

5

In fact, this is exactly what the
\beta
- and
\eta
-rules of the transpension type say (when
\Delta
is empty).
which is equivalent to the right adjoint
{\between}[{u}]
being fully faithful
[
nLa23c
]
, whence the section title.

However, the current typing rules become unusable in a more general setting, as well as in more specific settings where we may start adding operations that we need in important applications.
First, we have no story for substitutions which exist in cubical type systems such as endpoints
(0/i):\Gamma\to(\Gamma,i:{\mathbb{I}})

[
BCM15
,
BCH14
,
CCHM17
]
or connections
(j\wedge k/i):(\Gamma,j,k:{\mathbb{I}})\to(\Gamma,i:{\mathbb{I}})

[
CCHM17
]
, as there is no formation rule for
{\between}[{0}]\,A
or
{\between}[{j\wedge k}]\,A
.
Secondly, in non-fully-faithful generalizations featuring the contraction rule for shape variables, the transpension is not stable under substitution of the shape variables preceding
u
, so in those settings the way we internalized the transpension type here was too naïve.
6
6

6

Indeed, write
\Omega[{u}]
for the operation of cartesian weakening over a shape variable
u:{\mathbb{U}}
, which is an example of a substitution involving shape variables. If in general
\Omega[{u}]\circ{\between}[{v}]\cong{\between}[{v}]\circ\Omega[{u}]
, then by uniqueness of the left adjoint we would find that
\Pi\,{v}\circ\Sigma\,{u}\cong\Sigma\,{u}\circ\Pi\,{v}
. This is clearly false for cartesian shapes such as the interval
{\mathbb{I}}
in HoTT. For more information on how the transpension type commutes with other operations, see the technical report
[
Nuy23b
]
.

In order to obtain a type system that does not fail in the presence of endpoints, connections or shape variable contraction, in the rest of the paper we will rely on MTT, which we briefly summarize in
section

3
.

2.2.
Poles

We can still try to get a grasp on
{\between}[{0}]\,A
in cubical type systems, however.
In general we have
T[0/i]\cong(\forall\,{i}.(i\equiv_{{\mathbb{I}}}0)\to T)
.
Assuming
T={\between}[{i}]\,A
and
\mathsf{oneIsNotZero}:(1\equiv_{{\mathbb{I}}}0)\to{\mathsf{Empty}}
, the latter type is inhabited by

\mathsf{pole}_{0}:=\lambda i.\lambda e.\mathsf{mer}[{i}]\,\mathopen{}\left(\mathsf{case}\,\mathopen{}\left(\mathsf{oneIsNotZero}~\mathopen{}\left(({\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}i.e)~1\right)\mathclose{}\right)\mathclose{}\,\mathsf{of}\mathopen{}\left\{\begin{array}[]{l c l}\end{array}\right\}\mathclose{}\right)\mathclose{}:\forall\,{i}.(i\equiv_{{\mathbb{I}}}0)\to{\between}[{i}]\,A

where
{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}i.e
has type
\forall\,{i}.(i\equiv_{{\mathbb{I}}}0)
.
Moreover, using the
\eta
-rules for functions and the transpension type and a (provable propositional)
\eta
-rule for
{\mathsf{Empty}}
, we can show that this is the only element.
Thus we see that the transpension type essentially consists of one meridian
(i:{\mathbb{I}})\to{\between}[{i}]\,T
for every
t:T
, and that these meridians are all equal to
\mathsf{pole}_{0}
at
i=0
and analogously to
\mathsf{pole}_{1}
at
i=1
.
This makes the transpension type quite reminiscent of a dependent version of the suspension type from HoTT
[
Uni13
]
, although the quantification of the context in the formation and construction rules is obviously a distinction.

2.3.
Internal transposition

We can internally show that the following types are isomorphic:
7
7

7

This statement of internal transposition is not parallel to the general MTT one (
proposition

3.4
). The current variation is provable from the general result because the right adjoint
{\between}[{u}]
is fully faithful.

(\forall({u:{\mathbb{U}}}).A)\to B\quad\cong\quad\forall({u:{\mathbb{U}}}).(A\to{\between}[{u}]\,B).

Indeed, given
f:(\forall({u:{\mathbb{U}}}).A)\to B
, we can define
g:\forall({u:{\mathbb{U}}}).(A\to{\between}[{u}]\,B)
by

g\,u\,a=\mathsf{mer}[{u}]\,(f\,({\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}u.a)).

Conversely, given
g:\forall({u:{\mathbb{U}}}).(A\to{\between}[{u}]\,B)
, we can define
f:(\forall({u:{\mathbb{U}}}).A)\to B
by

f\,\hat{a}=\mathsf{unmer}\mathopen{}\left(u.g\,u\,(\hat{a}\,u)\right)\mathclose{}.

These constructions are mutually inverse. Indeed, plugging the definition of
f
into that of
g
, we find in context
(\Gamma,u:{\mathbb{U}},a:A)
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{mer}[{u}]\,\mathopen{}\left(\mathsf{unmer}(u.g\,u\,(({\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}u.a)\,u))\right)\mathclose{}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{mer}[{u}]\,\mathopen{}\left(\mathsf{unmer}(u.(g\,u\,a)[u/u,({\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}u.(a))\,u/(a)])\right)\mathclose{}=g\,u\,a

using the
\eta
-rule of the transpension type. Conversely, plugging
g
into
f
, we find in context
(\Gamma,\hat{a}:\forall\,{u}.A)
:

\displaystyle\mathrel{\phantom{=}}\mathsf{unmer}\mathopen{}\left(u.\kern 5.0pt(\mathsf{mer}[{u}]\,(f\,({\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}u.a)))[u/u,\hat{a}\,u/a]\kern 5.0pt\right)\mathclose{}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{unmer}\mathopen{}\left(u.(\mathsf{mer}[{u}]\,(\kern 5.0pt(f\,({\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}u.a))[{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}u.(\hat{a}\,u)/{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}u.(a)]\kern 5.0pt))\right)\mathclose{}

(
)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{unmer}\mathopen{}\left(u.(\mathsf{mer}[{u}]\,(\kern 5.0pt(f\,(\lambda u.\hat{a}\,u))\kern 5.0pt))\right)\mathclose{}

(
corollary

2.4
)

\displaystyle=f\,(\lambda u.(\hat{a}\,u))=f\,\hat{a}.

(
)

2.4.
Higher-dimensional pattern matching

Now that we know internally that
\forall\,{u}
is a left adjoint (with internal right adjoint
{\between}[{u}]
), we can proceed to conclude that it preserves colimits, e.g. we can show
i:(\forall\,{u}.A\uplus B)\cong(\forall\,{u}.A)\uplus(\forall\,{u}.B)
.
The map to the left is trivially defined by case analysis.
The map to the right is equivalent by transposition to a function
\forall\,{u}.(A\uplus B\to{\between}[{u}]\,((\forall\,{v}.A[v/u])\uplus(\forall\,{v}.B[v/u])))
.
This is in turn constructed by case analysis from the transpositions of the coproduct’s constructors
\mathsf{inl}
and
\mathsf{inr}
.

By straightforward application of
section

2.3
, the transpositions of the constructors are:

\displaystyle\lambda u.\lambda a.\mathsf{mer}[{u}]\,(\mathsf{inl}\,({\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}u.a))

\displaystyle:\forall\,{u}.\mathopen{}\left(A\to{\between}[{u}]\,((\forall\,{v}.A[v/u])\uplus(\forall\,{v}.B[v/u]))\right)\mathclose{}

\displaystyle\lambda u.\lambda b.\mathsf{mer}[{u}]\,(\mathsf{inr}\,({\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}u.b))

\displaystyle:\forall\,{u}.\mathopen{}\left(B\to{\between}[{u}]\,((\forall\,{v}.A[v/u])\uplus(\forall\,{v}.B[v/u]))\right)\mathclose{}

Pasting these together, we get

\displaystyle\lambda u.\lambda c.\mathsf{case}\,c\,\mathsf{of}\mathopen{}\left\{\begin{array}[]{l c l}\mathsf{inl}\,a&\mapsto&\mathsf{mer}[{u}]\,(\mathsf{inl}\,({\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}u.a))\\
\mathsf{inr}\,b&\mapsto&\mathsf{mer}[{u}]\,(\mathsf{inr}\,({\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}u.b))\end{array}\right\}\mathclose{}

\displaystyle:\forall\,{u}.\mathopen{}\left(A\uplus B\to{\between}[{u}]\,((\forall\,{v}.A[v/u])\uplus(\forall\,{v}.B[v/u]))\right)\mathclose{}.

Transposing again as in
section

2.3
, we find

\displaystyle i:(\forall\,{u}.A\uplus B)\to(\forall\,{u}.A)\uplus(\forall\,{u}.B)

\displaystyle i\,\hat{c}=\mathsf{unmer}\,\mathopen{}\left(u.\mathsf{case}\,\hat{c}\,u\,\mathsf{of}\mathopen{}\left\{\begin{array}[]{l c l}\mathsf{inl}\,a&\mapsto&\mathsf{mer}[{u}]\,(\mathsf{inl}\,({\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}u.a))\\
\mathsf{inr}\,b&\mapsto&\mathsf{mer}[{u}]\,(\mathsf{inr}\,({\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}u.b))\end{array}\right\}\mathclose{}\right)\mathclose{}.

Let us consider our categorically motivated creation from a more type-theoretical perspective.
We obtain an argument
\hat{c}:\forall\,{u}.A\uplus B
which we would like to pattern match on, in order to create an element of type
(\forall\,{u}.A)\uplus(\forall\,{u}.B)
.
Of course we cannot pattern match on a function, so we call the
\mathsf{unmer}
constructor which brings
u:{\mathbb{U}}
in scope and changes the goal to
{\between}[{u}]\,((\forall\,{v}.A[v/u])\uplus(\forall\,{v}.B[v/u]))
.
We can then reduce
\hat{c}
by one dimension by applying it to
u
, allowing a case analysis.
The first case brings in scope
a:A
(and the second case will be analogous), so we are in context
(\Gamma,\hat{c}:\forall\,{u}.A\uplus B,u:{\mathbb{U}},a:A)
.
We then use the meridian constructor, which again removes
u
from scope, turns
a:A
into a function
{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}u.a:\forall\,{u}.A
and again reduces the goal to
(\forall\,{u}.A)\uplus(\forall\,{u}.B)
, so that
\mathsf{inl}
completes the proof.
We have essentially pattern matched on a higher-dimensional object!

Let us now check that
i
is indeed inverse to the trivial implementation of
i^{-1}
. We have:

\displaystyle\mathrel{\phantom{=}}(i\circ i^{-1})(\mathsf{inl}\,\hat{a})=i(\lambda u.\mathsf{inl}\,({\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}u.a))

\displaystyle=\mathsf{unmer}\mathopen{}\left(u.\kern 5.0pt\mathopen{}\left(\mathsf{mer}[{u}]\,(\mathsf{inl}\,({\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}u.a))\right)\mathclose{}[u/u,\hat{a}\,u/a]\kern 5.0pt\right)\mathclose{}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{unmer}\mathopen{}\left(u.\mathopen{}\left(\mathsf{mer}[{u}]\,(\mathsf{inl}\kern 5.0pt({\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}u.a)[{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}u.\hat{a}\,u/{\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}u.a]\kern 5.0pt)\right)\mathclose{}\right)\mathclose{}

(
)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{unmer}\mathopen{}\left(u.\mathopen{}\left(\mathsf{mer}[{u}]\,(\mathsf{inl}\,(\lambda u.\hat{a}\,u))\right)\mathclose{}\right)\mathclose{}

(
corollary

2.4
)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{inl}\,\hat{a}.

(
)

and similar for
(i\circ i^{-1})(\mathsf{inr}\,\hat{b})
.
Using the technique of higher dimensional pattern matching just developed, we can prove the other equation also by pattern matching! By similar steps as before, we have:

\displaystyle(i^{-1}\circ i)(\lambda u.\mathsf{inl}\,(\hat{a}\,u))

\displaystyle=i^{-1}(\mathsf{unmer}(u.\kern 5.0pt(\mathsf{mer}[{u}]\,(\mathsf{inl}\,({\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}u.a)))[u/u,\hat{a}\,u/a]\kern 5.0pt))

\displaystyle=i^{-1}(\mathsf{unmer}(u.(\mathsf{mer}[{u}]\,(\mathsf{inl}\,\hat{a}))))=i^{-1}(\mathsf{inl}\,\hat{a})=\lambda u.\mathsf{inl}\,(\hat{a}\,u),

and a similar result for
(i^{-1}\circ i)(\lambda u.\mathsf{inr}\,(\hat{b}\,u))
.

3.
Multimode Type Theory

As announced, we will rely on the extensional version of Gratzer et al.’s multimode and multimodal dependent type system MTT
[
GKNB21
,
GKNB20a
]
in order to frame the transpension and its left adjoints as modal operators.
We refer to the original work for details, but give a brief overview in the current section.
In
section

3.4
, we decorate the usual MTT notation with reminders of the modalities’ semantic left adjoints, which are syntactically obscured by the lock notation.

3.1.
The mode theory

MTT is parametrized by a
mode theory
, which is a strict 2-category whose objects, morphisms and 2-cells we will refer to as
modes
,
modalities
and, well,
2-cells
respectively. Semantically, every mode
p
will correspond to an entire model of dependent type theory
\mathopen{}\left\llbracket p\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}
. A modality
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}:p\to q
will consist of a functor
\mathopen{}\left\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}}\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}:\mathopen{}\left\llbracket q\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}\to\mathopen{}\left\llbracket p\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}
acting on contexts and substitutions, and an operation
\mathopen{}\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}
that is almost a dependent right adjoint (DRA
[
BCM+20
]
) to
\mathopen{}\left\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}}\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}
; for all our purposes it will be an actual DRA and even one arising from a weak CwF morphism
[
BCM+20
, lemma 17]
[
Nuy18a
]
. A 2-cell
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\alpha}:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\nu}
is interpreted as a natural transformation
\mathopen{}\left\llbracket{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\alpha}}\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}:\mathopen{}\left\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\nu}}\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}\to\mathopen{}\left\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}}\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}
and hence also gives rise to an appropriate transformation
\mathopen{}\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\alpha}\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}:\mathopen{}\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}\to\mathopen{}\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\nu}\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}
.

3.2.
Judgement forms

The judgement forms of MTT are listed in
fig.

2
.
All forms are annotated with a mode
p
which specifies in what category they are to be interpreted.
Every judgement form also has a corresponding equality judgement, which is respected by everything as the typing rules are to be read as a specification of a generalized algebraic theory (GAT
[
Car86
,
AK16
]
).
The statements
p\,\mathsf{mode}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}:p\to q
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\alpha}:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\nu}
are simply requirements about the mode theory. This means we give no syntax or equality rules for modalities and 2-cells: these are fixed by the choice of mode theory.

Judgement forms:

\displaystyle p\mathrel{|}\Gamma\,\mathsf{ctx}

\Gamma
is a context at mode
p
,

\displaystyle p\mathrel{|}\sigma:\Delta\to\Gamma

\sigma
is a simultaneous substitution from
\Delta
to
\Gamma
at mode
p
,

\displaystyle p\mathrel{|}\Gamma\vdash T\,\mathsf{type}

T
is a type in context
\Gamma
at mode
p
,

\displaystyle p\mathrel{|}\Gamma\vdash t:T

t
has type
T
in context
\Gamma
at mode
p
.

Figure 2.
Judgement forms of MTT
[
GKNB21
]
.

3.3.
Typing rules

The typing rules are listed in
figs.

3
,
5
,
4
and
6

and discussed below.

3.3.1.
The type theory at each mode

The type theory at each mode:

Basic rules of dependent type theory (including all desired types) at each mode
q
, e.g.:

\dfrac{\begin{array}[]{l l}\hskip-4.30554pt\text{\hypertarget{rule:ctx-empty}{{ctx-empty}}}\\
q\,\mathsf{mode}\end{array}\hfill}{\begin{array}[]{l l}q\mathrel{|}\cdot\,\mathsf{ctx}\end{array}\hfill}\text{\text{}}\qquad\dfrac{\begin{array}[]{l l}\hskip-4.30554pt\text{\hypertarget{rule:ctx-ext}{{ctx-ext}}}\\
q\mathrel{|}\Gamma\vdash T\,\mathsf{type}\end{array}\hfill}{\begin{array}[]{l l}q\mathrel{|}\Gamma,x:T\,\mathsf{ctx}\end{array}\hfill}\text{\text{}}\qquad\dfrac{\begin{array}[]{l l}\hskip-4.30554pt\text{\hypertarget{rule:ctx-ext:intro}{{ctx-ext:intro}}}\\
q\mathrel{|}\sigma:\Delta\to\Gamma\quad q\mathrel{|}\Delta\vdash t:T[\sigma]\end{array}\hfill}{\begin{array}[]{l l}q\mathrel{|}(\sigma,t/x):\Delta\to(\Gamma,x:T)\\
\mathrm{where}\,\,\tau=((x/\oslash)\circ\tau,x[\tau]/x)\\
\phantom{\mathrm{where}}\,\,(\sigma,t/x)\circ\rho=(\sigma\circ\rho,t[\rho]/x)\end{array}\hfill}\text{\text{}}

\dfrac{\begin{array}[]{l l}\hskip-4.30554pt\text{\hypertarget{rule:ctx-ext:wkn}{{ctx-ext:wkn}}}\\
q\mathrel{|}\Gamma\,\mathsf{ctx}\qquad q\mathrel{|}\Gamma\vdash T\,\mathsf{type}\end{array}\hfill}{\begin{array}[]{l l}q\mathrel{|}(x/\oslash):(\Gamma,x:T)\to\Gamma\\
\mathrm{where}\,\,(x/\oslash)\circ(\sigma,t/x)=\sigma\end{array}\hfill}\text{\text{}}\quad\dfrac{\begin{array}[]{l l}\hskip-4.30554pt\text{\hypertarget{rule:ctx-ext:var}{{ctx-ext:var}}}\\
q\mathrel{|}\Gamma\,\mathsf{ctx}\qquad q\mathrel{|}\Gamma\vdash T\,\mathsf{type}\end{array}\hfill}{\begin{array}[]{l l}q\mathrel{|}\Gamma,x:T\vdash x:T[(x/\oslash)]\\
\mathrm{where}\,\,x[\sigma,t/x]=t\end{array}\hfill}\text{\text{}}\quad\dfrac{\begin{array}[]{l l}\hskip-4.30554pt\text{\hypertarget{rule:sigma}{{sigma}}}\\
q\mathrel{|}\Gamma\vdash A\,\mathsf{type}\\
q\mathrel{|}\Gamma,x:A\vdash B\,\mathsf{type}\end{array}\hfill}{\begin{array}[]{l l}q\mathrel{|}\Gamma\vdash(x:A)\times B\,\mathsf{type}\end{array}\hfill}\text{\text{}}

\dfrac{\begin{array}[]{l l}\hskip-4.30554pt\text{\hypertarget{rule:uni}{{uni}}}\\
q\mathrel{|}\Gamma\,\mathsf{ctx}\quad\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}\end{array}\hfill}{\begin{array}[]{l l}q\mathrel{|}\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{U}_{\ell}^{q}\,\mathsf{type}_{\ell+1}\end{array}\hfill}\text{\text{}}\qquad\dfrac{\begin{array}[]{l l}\hskip-4.30554pt\text{\hypertarget{rule:uni:elim}{{uni:elim}}}\\
q\mathrel{|}\Gamma\vdash t:\mathsf{U}_{\ell}^{q}\end{array}\hfill}{\begin{array}[]{l l}q\mathrel{|}\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{El}(t)\,\mathsf{type}_{\ell}\\
\mathrm{where}\,\,\mathsf{El}(\ulcorner T\urcorner)=T\end{array}\hfill}\text{\text{}}\qquad\dfrac{\begin{array}[]{l l}\hskip-4.30554pt\text{\hypertarget{rule:uni:intro}{{uni:intro}}}\\
q\mathrel{|}\Gamma\vdash T\,\mathsf{type}_{\ell}\end{array}\hfill}{\begin{array}[]{l l}q\mathrel{|}\Gamma\vdash\ulcorner T\urcorner:\mathsf{U}_{\ell}^{q}\\
\mathrm{where}\,\,\ulcorner\mathsf{El}(t)\urcorner=t\end{array}\hfill}\text{\text{}}

Figure 3.
MTT includes all rules of ordinary DTT at each mode.

Since every mode corresponds to a model of all of dependent type theory (DTT), we start by importing
all the usual typing rules of DTT
, to be applied in MTT at any given fixed mode.
Some examples of such rules are given in
fig.

3
, where we have consciously included rules for non-modal context extension, even though these will be generalized to modal rules later on.
One reason to do so is that other rules of DTT, such as
, depend on these and therefore cannot be imported without.
Another is that this way, we have a warm-up towards the modal rules and in particular we can make a point about de Bruijn indices.
Although variables in
fig.

3
are named, the rules
and
effectively enforce a
de Bruijn
discipline, where we can only name the last variable in the context and have to weaken explicitly if it is deeper down, e.g.

x:A,y:B,z:C\vdash x[(y/\oslash)][(z/\oslash)]:A[(x/\oslash)][(y/\oslash)][(z/\oslash)].

We take the viewpoint
8
8

8

as is done in the MTT technical report
[
GKNB20a
]
; the paper
[
GKNB21
]
speaks from a more implementation-oriented perspective.
that the official system is unnamed and uses this substitution-based de Bruijn discipline to refer to variables.
In order to improve human communication, we will name variables anyway and use the resulting redundancy to leave weakening substitutions implicit unambiguously.
This allows for the following unofficial admissible ‘rule’

\dfrac{\begin{array}[]{l l}\hskip-4.30554pt\text{\hypertarget{rule:ctx-ext:var:lookup}{{ctx-ext:var:lookup}}}\\
q\mathrel{|}\Gamma,x:T,\Delta\,\mathsf{ctx}\end{array}\hfill}{\begin{array}[]{l l}q\mathrel{|}\Gamma,x:T,\Delta\vdash x:T\end{array}\hfill}\text{\text{}}.

Furthermore, we use other common notational conventions such as writing
(t/x)
instead of
({\mathrm{id}}_{\Gamma},t/x):\Gamma\to(\Gamma,x:T)
.

We assume that DTT has a
universe
à la Coquand with mutually inverse encoding and decoding operations (which we will henceforth suppress), and we ignore cumulativity-related hassle, referring to Gratzer et al.
[
GKNB21
]
for details.

3.3.2.
Modal types, part 1

\begin{array}[]{l}\dfrac{\begin{array}[]{l l}\hskip-4.30554pt\text{\hypertarget{rule:wdra}{{wdra}}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}:p\to q\\
p\mathrel{|}\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}}\vdash A\,\mathsf{type}_{\ell}\end{array}\hfill}{\begin{array}[]{l l}q\mathrel{|}\Gamma\vdash\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}\mathrel{|}A\right\rangle\mathclose{}\,\mathsf{type}_{\ell}\end{array}\hfill}\text{\text{}}\\
\rule{0.0pt}{51.6665pt}\dfrac{\begin{array}[]{l l}\hskip-4.30554pt\text{\hypertarget{rule:wdra:intro}{{wdra:intro}}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}:p\to q\\
p\mathrel{|}\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}}\vdash a:A\end{array}\hfill}{\begin{array}[]{l l}q\mathrel{|}\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{mod}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}}\,a:\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}\mathrel{|}A\right\rangle\mathclose{}\end{array}\hfill}\text{\text{}}\end{array}\quad\begin{array}[]{l}\dfrac{\begin{array}[]{l l}\hskip-4.30554pt\text{\hypertarget{rule:wdra:elim}{{wdra:elim}}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}:p\to q\quad{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\nu}:q\to r\\
q\mathrel{|}\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\nu}}\vdash\hat{a}:\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}\mathrel{|}A\right\rangle\mathclose{}\\
r\mathrel{|}\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\nu}\shortmid\hat{x}:\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}\mathrel{|}A\right\rangle\mathclose{}\vdash C\,\mathsf{type}\\
r\mathrel{|}\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\nu\circ\mu}\shortmid x:A\vdash c:C[\mathsf{mod}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}}\,x/\hat{x}]\end{array}\hfill}{\begin{array}[]{l l}r\mathrel{|}\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{let}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\nu}}\,(\mathsf{mod}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}}\,x=\hat{a})\,\mathsf{in}\,c:C[\hat{a}/\hat{x}]\\
\mathrm{where}\,\,\mathsf{let}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\nu}}\,(\mathsf{mod}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}}\,x=\mathsf{mod}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}}\,a)\,\mathsf{in}\,c=c[a/x]\end{array}\hfill}\text{\text{}}\end{array}

Before proceeding to the MTT-specific structural rules, let us first have a look at the formation and introduction rules
and
of
modal types

\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}\mathrel{|}A\right\rangle\mathclose{}
in
fig.

4
.
These are not unlike the formation and introduction rules of the transpension type in
fig.

1
and work by transposition: we apply the left adjoint of the modality
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}
(in the form of a lock) to the premise’s context. As such, they behave like DRAs, but their elimination rule
(which we consider later) is weaker, so we call them weak DRAs.

3.3.3.
Structural rules

Context locking:

Note: We write
(\sigma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}})
as shorthand for
(\sigma,{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}1}:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}})
, an instance of
.

\dfrac{\begin{array}[]{l l}\hskip-3.87498pt\text{\hypertarget{rule:lock}{{lock}}}\\
q\mathrel{|}\Gamma\,\mathsf{ctx}\hskip 9.24994pt{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}:p\to q\end{array}\hfill}{\begin{array}[]{l l}p\mathrel{|}\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}}\,\mathsf{ctx}\\
\mathrm{where}\,\,\Gamma=(\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}1}})\\
\phantom{\mathrm{where}}\,\,(\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\nu}},\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}})=(\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\nu\circ\mu}})\end{array}\hfill}\text{\text{}}\hskip 18.49988pt\dfrac{\begin{array}[]{l l}\hskip-3.87498pt\text{\hypertarget{rule:lock:fmap}{{lock:fmap}}}\\
q\mathrel{|}\sigma:\Gamma\to\Delta\hskip 18.49988pt{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu,\nu}:p\to q\hskip 18.49988pt{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\alpha}:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\nu}\end{array}\hfill}{\begin{array}[]{l l}p\mathrel{|}(\sigma,{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\alpha}}):(\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\nu}})\to(\Delta,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}})\\
\mathrm{where}\,\,\sigma=(\sigma,{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}1}:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}1}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}1}})\hskip 9.24994pt(\sigma,{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\alpha^{\prime}}},{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\alpha}})=(\sigma,{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}(\alpha^{\prime}\mathbin{\star}\alpha)}})\\
\phantom{\mathrm{where}}\,\,1=(1,{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}1}:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}})\hskip 9.24994pt(\sigma,{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\alpha}})\circ(\tau,{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\beta}})=(\sigma\circ\tau,{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}(\beta\circ\alpha)}})\end{array}\hfill}\text{\text{}}

Modal context extension:

We consider the non-modal rule
and its introduction, elimination and computation rules as a special case of
for
p=q
and
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}={\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}1}
.

\dfrac{\begin{array}[]{l l}\hskip-4.30554pt\text{\hypertarget{rule:ctx-modext}{{ctx-modext}}}\\
q\mathrel{|}\Gamma\,\mathsf{ctx}\quad{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}:p\to q\\
p\mathrel{|}\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}}\vdash T\,\mathsf{type}\end{array}\hfill}{\begin{array}[]{l l}q\mathrel{|}\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}\shortmid x:T\,\mathsf{ctx}\end{array}\hfill}\text{\text{}}\qquad\dfrac{\begin{array}[]{l l}\hskip-4.30554pt\text{\hypertarget{rule:ctx-modext:intro}{{ctx-modext:intro}}}\\
q\mathrel{|}\sigma:\Delta\to\Gamma\qquad{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}:p\to q\\
p\mathrel{|}\Delta,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}}\vdash t:T[\sigma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}}]\end{array}\hfill}{\begin{array}[]{l l}q\mathrel{|}(\sigma,t/x):\Delta\to(\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}\shortmid x:T)\\
\mathrm{where}\,\,\tau=((x/\oslash)\circ\tau,x[\tau,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}}]/x)\\
\phantom{\mathrm{where}}\,\,(\sigma,t/x)\circ\rho=(\sigma\circ\rho,t[\rho,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}}]/x)\end{array}\hfill}\text{\text{}}

\quad\dfrac{\begin{array}[]{l l}\hskip-4.30554pt\text{\hypertarget{rule:ctx-modext:wkn}{{ctx-modext:wkn}}}\\
q\mathrel{|}\Gamma\,\mathsf{ctx}\qquad{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}:p\to q\\
p\mathrel{|}\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}}\vdash T\,\mathsf{type}\end{array}\hfill}{\begin{array}[]{l l}q\mathrel{|}(x/\oslash):(\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}\shortmid x:T)\to\Gamma\\
\mathrm{where}\,\,(x/\oslash)\circ(\sigma,t/x)=\sigma\end{array}\hfill}\text{\text{}}\qquad\dfrac{\begin{array}[]{l l}\hskip-4.30554pt\text{\hypertarget{rule:ctx-modext:var}{{ctx-modext:var}}}\\
q\mathrel{|}\Gamma\,\mathsf{ctx}&{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}:p\to q\\
p\mathrel{|}\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}}\vdash T\,\mathsf{type}\end{array}\hfill}{\begin{array}[]{l l}q\mathrel{|}\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}\shortmid x:T,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}}\vdash x:T[(x/\oslash),\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}}]\\
\mathrm{where}\,\,\Delta,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}}\vdash x[\sigma,t/x,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}}]=t:T[\sigma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}}]\end{array}\hfill}\text{\text{}}

The structural rules of MTT are listed in
fig.

5
.
Context formation
starts with the empty context which exists at any mode, and proceeds by adding locks and variables.

Adding
locks
(
) is strictly functorial: it preserves identity and composition of modalities. In fact, it is strictly 2-functorial: it also has an action on 2-cells (
, producing substitutions between locked contexts) that preserves identity and composition of 2-cells. It is also strictly bifunctorial: we can combine a substitution and a 2-cell to a substitution between locked contexts. If the 2-cell is the identity, then we write
\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}}
for
{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}1}:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}}
.

A
modal variable

{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}\shortmid x:T
introduced by
is essentially the same as a non-modal variable
\hat{x}:\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}\mathrel{|}T\right\rangle\mathclose{}
(which in turn is shorthand for
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}1}\shortmid\hat{x}:\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}\mathrel{|}T\right\rangle\mathclose{}
), but the judgemental modal annotation allows direct access to a variable of type
A
through the variable rule.
Hence, the type
T
is checked the same way as it would be in
\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}\mathrel{|}T\right\rangle\mathclose{}
.
Terms
t
substituted for a modal variable
x
are also checked in the locked context, as if we would be substituting
\mathsf{mod}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}}\,t
instead.
The
variable
rule does not produce
x:\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}\mathrel{|}T\right\rangle\mathclose{}
but instead uses transposition to move the modality
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}
to the left in the form of a lock. As such, it can be seen as implicitly involving a co-unit.

By analogy with
, we would like a more general unofficial variable ‘rule’ that allows accessing a variable
x
that is buried under a general telescope
\Delta
rather than a single lock.
By
, we can weaken under locks (which, like ordinary weakening, we will leave notationally implicit), so we can easily remove all variables from
\Delta
and then apply strict functoriality of
to fuse the remaining locks, obtaining a single lock
\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mathsf{locks}(\Delta)}}
, where the modality
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mathsf{locks}(\Delta)}
is defined as follows:

\mathsf{locks}(\cdot)=1,\qquad\mathsf{locks}(\Delta,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}})=\mathsf{locks}(\Delta)\circ\mu,\qquad\mathsf{locks}(\Delta,{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}\shortmid x:T)=\mathsf{locks}(\Delta).

Then the only remaining thing we need is a 2-cell
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\alpha}:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mathsf{locks}(\Delta)}
.
This leads to the following admissible variable ‘rule’

\dfrac{\begin{array}[]{l l}\hskip-4.30554pt\text{\hypertarget{rule:ctx-modext:var:lookup}{{ctx-modext:var:lookup}}}\\
q\mathrel{|}\Gamma\,\mathsf{ctx}&{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}:p\to q\\
p\mathrel{|}\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}}\vdash T\,\mathsf{type}&{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\alpha}:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mathsf{locks}(\Delta)}\end{array}\hfill}{\begin{array}[]{l l}q\mathrel{|}\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}\shortmid x:T,\Delta\vdash x_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\alpha}}:T[{\mathrm{id}}_{\Gamma},{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\alpha}}]\end{array}\hfill}\text{\text{}}

where
x_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\alpha}}
is defined as
x[{\mathrm{id}}_{(\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}\shortmid x:T)},{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\alpha}:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mathsf{locks}(\Delta)}}]
, leaving the necessary weakenings under locks implicit.
Substitution is then given by

\displaystyle x_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\alpha}}[{\mathrm{id}}_{\Gamma},a/x,{\mathrm{id}}_{\Delta}]

\displaystyle=x[{\mathrm{id}}_{(\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}\shortmid x:T)},{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\alpha}}][{\mathrm{id}}_{\Gamma},a/x,{\mathrm{id}}_{\Delta}]

\displaystyle=x[{\mathrm{id}}_{\Gamma},a/x,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}}][{\mathrm{id}}_{\Gamma},{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\alpha}}]

\displaystyle=a[{\mathrm{id}}_{\Gamma},{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\alpha}}],

or more briefly
x_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\alpha}}[a/x]=a[{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\alpha}}]
.

3.3.4.
Modal types, part 2

Modal elimination
(
,
fig.

4
) uses a
let
-syntax to turn the modal type into a judgemental annotation on a variable.

3.3.5.
Modal function types

Modal
function type
formation and introduction (
fig.

6
) are by simple abstraction. Modal function application checks the argument in a locked context, just like modal variable substitution does.

\dfrac{\begin{array}[]{l l}\hskip-4.30554pt\text{\hypertarget{rule:modpi}{{modpi}}}\\
p\mathrel{|}\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}}\vdash A\,\mathsf{type}_{\ell}\quad{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}:p\to q\\
q\mathrel{|}\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}\shortmid x:A\vdash B\,\mathsf{type}_{\ell}\end{array}\hfill}{\begin{array}[]{l l}q\mathrel{|}\Gamma\vdash({\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}\shortmid x:A)\to B\,\mathsf{type}_{\ell}\end{array}\hfill}\text{\text{}}

\dfrac{\begin{array}[]{l l}\hskip-4.30554pt\text{\hypertarget{rule:modpi:intro}{{modpi:intro}}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}:p\to q\\
q\mathrel{|}\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}\shortmid x:A\vdash b:B\end{array}\hfill}{\begin{array}[]{l l}q\mathrel{|}\Gamma\vdash\lambda({\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}\shortmid x).b:({\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}\shortmid x:A)\to B\\
\mathrm{where}\,\,\lambda({\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}\shortmid x).(f\cdot_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}}x)=f\end{array}\hfill}\text{\text{}}\qquad\dfrac{\begin{array}[]{l l}\hskip-4.30554pt\text{\hypertarget{rule:modpi:elim}{{modpi:elim}}}\\
q\mathrel{|}\Gamma\vdash f:({\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}\shortmid x:A)\to B\\
p\mathrel{|}\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}}\vdash a:A\quad{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}:p\to q\end{array}\hfill}{\begin{array}[]{l l}q\mathrel{|}\Gamma\vdash f\cdot_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}}a:B[a/x]\\
\mathrm{where}\,\,(\lambda({\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}\shortmid x).b)\cdot_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}}a=b[a/x]\end{array}\hfill}\text{\text{}}

Typing rules for MTT’s modal

-types
