3.4.
Left adjoint reminders

In MTT, we can on one hand apply a modality
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}:p\to q
to a type
T
to obtain a modal type
\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}\mathrel{|}T\right\rangle\mathclose{}
, and on the other hand we can apply its left adjoint
\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}}
to a context
\Gamma
to obtain
\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}}
.
Type-theoretically, it is only sensible that the lock
\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}}
mentions
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}
for
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}
is a premise of the
rule.
From a semantic/categorical viewpoint however, the indirection of mentioning
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}
when you are actually talking about some left adjoint functor
K=\mathopen{}\left\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}}\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}
to
M=\mathopen{}\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}
can really get in the way of understanding what is going on.

For example, in
section

6
, we will come up with a modality
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}{\between}[{u}]}
for the transpension, and then the introduction rule
will take the form

\dfrac{\begin{array}[]{l l}p\mathrel{|}\Delta,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}{\between}[{u}]}}\vdash t:T\end{array}\hfill}{\begin{array}[]{l l}q\mathrel{|}\Delta\vdash\mathsf{mod}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}{\between}[{u}]}}\,t:\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}{\between}[{u}]}\mathrel{|}T\right\rangle\mathclose{}\end{array}\hfill}\text{}

for certain modes
p
and
q
, which is quite reminiscent of the introduction rule of the transpension type in FFTraS (
section

2
)
if you bear in mind
that
\mathopen{}\left\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}{\between}[{u}]}}\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}
is essentially
\forall\,{u}
.

Instead of littering this paper with remarks of the form ‘recall that
\mathopen{}\left\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}}\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}=\ldots
’, we will decorate locks, keys and variables with superscript reminders of the left adjoints to the modalities (and 2-cells mediating them) that are already in subscript.
Concretely, we will assign:

•

to every modality
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}:p\to q
a
left name

{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\kappa}:q\to p
(writing this succinctly as
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\kappa}\mathrel{\reflectbox{$\Vdash$}}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}:p\to q
),

•

to every 2-cell
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\alpha}:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu^{\prime}}
a left name
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\omega}:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\kappa^{\prime}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\kappa}
(where
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\kappa}\mathrel{\reflectbox{$\Vdash$}}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\kappa^{\prime}}\mathrel{\reflectbox{$\Vdash$}}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu^{\prime}}
; writing succinctly
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\kappa}\Leftarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\kappa^{\prime}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\omega}\mathrel{\reflectbox{$\vDash$}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\alpha}:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu^{\prime}}
).

Of course if
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\kappa^{\prime}}\mathrel{\reflectbox{$\Vdash$}}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu^{\prime}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\kappa}\mathrel{\reflectbox{$\Vdash$}}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}
, then the composite will be
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\kappa\circ\kappa^{\prime}}\mathrel{\reflectbox{$\Vdash$}}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu^{\prime}\circ\mu}
.
If a modality
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}
has a left adjoint
modality

{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\nu}
, then we will always use
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\nu}
as the left name of
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}
, and similar for 2-cells.
Then, we can write
\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\kappa}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}}
for
\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}}
, and
{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\omega}:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\kappa^{\prime}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\kappa}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\alpha}:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu^{\prime}}}
or just
{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\omega}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\alpha}}
for
{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\alpha}:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu^{\prime}}}
, and
x^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\omega}:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\kappa^{\prime}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\kappa}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\alpha}:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu^{\prime}}}
or just
x^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\omega}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\alpha}}
for
x_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\alpha}:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu^{\prime}}}
.
Note that we have

\displaystyle(\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\kappa_{1}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu_{1}}},\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\kappa_{2}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu_{2}}})

\displaystyle=(\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\kappa_{2}\circ\kappa_{1}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu_{1}\circ\mu_{2}}}),

\displaystyle(\sigma,{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\omega_{1}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\alpha_{1}}},{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\omega_{2}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\alpha_{2}}})

\displaystyle=(\sigma,{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\omega_{2}\mathbin{\star}\omega_{1}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\alpha_{1}\mathbin{\star}\alpha_{2}}}),

\displaystyle a[{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\omega}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\alpha}}][{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\psi}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\beta}}]

\displaystyle=a[{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\omega\circ\psi}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\beta\circ\alpha}}].

(1)

3.5.
Results

We highlight some results about MTT that are relevant in the current paper.

Proposition 3.1
.

We have
\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}1}\mathrel{|}A\right\rangle\mathclose{}\cong A
and
\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\nu\circ\mu}\mathrel{|}A\right\rangle\mathclose{}\cong\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\nu}\mathrel{|}\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}\mathrel{|}A\right\rangle\mathclose{}\right\rangle\mathclose{}
.

Proposition 3.2
.

For any 2-cell
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\alpha}:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\nu}
, we have
\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}\mathrel{|}A\right\rangle\mathclose{}\to\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\nu}\mathrel{|}A[{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\alpha}}]\right\rangle\mathclose{}
.

Proposition 3.3

(Projection)
.

If
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\kappa}\dashv{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}
internal to the mode theory, with unit
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\eta}:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}1}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu\circ\kappa}
and co-unit
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\varepsilon}:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\kappa\circ\mu}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}1}
, then there is a function
\varepsilon:({\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\kappa}\shortmid{\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}\mathrel{|}A\right\rangle\mathclose{}})\to A[{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\varepsilon}}]
, satisfying a
\beta
- and (thanks to extensionality) an
\eta
-law:

\varepsilon\cdot_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\kappa}}(\mathsf{mod}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}}\,a)=a[{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\varepsilon}}],\qquad\hat{a}=\mathsf{mod}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}}\,(\varepsilon\cdot_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\kappa}}(\hat{a}[{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\eta}}])).

Combined with these rules,
\varepsilon
is equally expressive as the
\mathsf{let}
-eliminator for
\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}\mathrel{|}{\llcorner\hskip-1.72218pt\lrcorner}\right\rangle\mathclose{}
.

Proposition 3.4

(Internal transposition)
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\kappa}\dashv{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}
internal to the mode theory, with unit
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\eta}:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}1}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu\circ\kappa}
and co-unit
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\varepsilon}:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\kappa\circ\mu}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}1}
.
Adding left names, we get
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\zeta}\mathrel{\reflectbox{$\Vdash$}}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\kappa}\dashv{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}
with
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}1}\Leftarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\zeta\circ\kappa}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\varepsilon^{\prime}}\mathrel{\reflectbox{$\vDash$}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\eta}:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}1}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu\circ\kappa}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\kappa\circ\zeta}\Leftarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}1}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\eta^{\prime}}\mathrel{\reflectbox{$\vDash$}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\varepsilon}:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\kappa\circ\mu}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}1}
.

Then there is an isomorphism of contexts expressing that
\kappa
respects context extension:

\displaystyle\sigma=(x^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\varepsilon^{\prime}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\eta}}/y)

\displaystyle:(\Gamma,x:A,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\kappa}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}})\cong(\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\kappa}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}},{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\kappa}\shortmid y:A[{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\varepsilon^{\prime}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\eta}}]).

The inverse is given by:

\textstyle{(\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\kappa}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}},{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\kappa}\shortmid y:A[{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\varepsilon^{\prime}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\eta}}])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\displaystyle({\mathrm{id}}_{(\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\kappa}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}},y)},{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\eta^{\prime}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\varepsilon}})}
\textstyle{(\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\kappa}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}},{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\kappa}\shortmid y:A[{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\varepsilon^{\prime}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\eta}}],\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\zeta}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\kappa}},\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\kappa}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\displaystyle(1_{\Gamma},{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\varepsilon^{\prime}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\eta}},y/x,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\kappa}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}})}
\textstyle{(\Gamma,x:A,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\kappa}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}})}

Correspondingly, given
B
in the codomain context of
\sigma
, there is an isomorphism of types

\displaystyle(x:A)\to\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}\mathrel{|}B[\sigma]\right\rangle\mathclose{}\qquad\cong\qquad\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}\mathrel{|}({\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\kappa}\shortmid y:A[{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\varepsilon^{\prime}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\eta}}])\to B\right\rangle\mathclose{}

expressing internal transposition.

4.
The Modal Transpension System (MTraS): General Mode Theory and Semantics

As mentioned in
section

1.3
, in our modal transpension system (MTraS), the transpension modality
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}{\between}[{u}]}
will be part of an adjoint triple of internal modalities
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{u}]}\dashv{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Pi\,{u}}\dashv{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}{\between}[{u}]}
together with weakening
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{u}]}
and universal quantification
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Pi\,{u}}
or, more generally in potentially non-cartesian systems, an adjoint triple
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Finv[{u}]}\dashv{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\forall\,{u}}\dashv{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}{\between}[{u}]}
together with fresh weakening
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Finv[{u}]}
and substructural (e.g. linear/affine) universal quantification
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\forall\,{u}}
.
The further left adjoints
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\Sigma\,{u}}
(cartesian) or
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\exists\,{u}}
(potentially non-cartesian) cannot be internalized because every internal MTT modality needs to have a further semantic left adjoint; thus, they will only appear as left names.

Notably, the aforementioned modalities all bind or depend on a variable, a phenomenon which is not supported by MTT.
We shall address this issue in the current section by grouping shape variables such as
u:{\mathbb{U}}
in a
shape context
which is not considered part of the type-theoretic context but instead serves as the
mode
of the judgement.

We assume that there are no prior modalities, i.e. that the type system to which we wish to add a transpension type is non-modal in the sense that it has a single mode and only the identity modality.
We assume that this single prior mode is modelled by the presheaf category
\mathrm{Psh}({\mathcal{W}})
.
Prior modalities and in particular their commutation with the modalities mentioned above, are considered in the technical report
[
Nuy23b
]
.

4.1.
Shape contexts

Assume we have in the prior system a context
{\mathbb{X}}
modelled by a presheaf
\Xi
over
{\mathcal{W}}
.
Then the presheaves
\mathrm{Psh}({\mathcal{W}}/\Xi)
over the category of elements
{\mathcal{W}}/\Xi
of the presheaf
\Xi
are also a model of dependent type theory.
Denoting the judgements of the latter system with a prefix
{\mathbb{X}}\mathrel{|}
, it happens to be the case that judgements
{\mathbb{X}}\mathrel{|}\Gamma\vdash J
(i.e.
\Gamma\vdash J
in
\mathrm{Psh}({\mathcal{W}}/\Xi)
) have precisely the same meaning as judgements
{\mathbb{X}}.\Gamma\vdash J
in
\mathrm{Psh}({\mathcal{W}})
(for a suitable but straightforward translation of
J
).
Thus, we will group together all shape variables (variables for which we want a transpension type) in a
shape context

{\mathbb{X}}
in front of the typing context.
Our judgements will then take the form
{\mathbb{X}}\mathrel{|}\Gamma\vdash J
.
Modal techniques will be used to signal what part of the context
\Gamma
is fresh for a shape variable
u:{\mathbb{U}}
, as this can then no longer be signalled by the position of
u:{\mathbb{U}}
in the context.
All of this allows us to frame the shape context
{\mathbb{X}}
as the
mode
of the judgement, as it determines the category
\mathrm{Psh}({\mathcal{W}}/\Xi)
in which the judgement is modelled.

Concretely, we fix a set of
shapes
and generate shape contexts by the following rules:

\dfrac{\begin{array}[]{l l}\hskip-4.30554pt\text{\hypertarget{rule:shp-ctx-empty}{{shp-ctx-empty}}}\\
\end{array}\hfill}{\begin{array}[]{l l}{\cdot}\,\mathsf{shpctx}\end{array}\hfill}\text{\text{}}\qquad\dfrac{\begin{array}[]{l l}\hskip-4.30554pt\text{\hypertarget{rule:shp-ctx-ext}{{shp-ctx-ext}}}\\
{\mathbb{X}}\,\mathsf{shpctx}\qquad{\mathbb{U}}\,\mathsf{shape}\end{array}\hfill}{\begin{array}[]{l l}{\mathbb{X}},u:{\mathbb{U}}\,\mathsf{shpctx}\end{array}\hfill}\text{\text{}}

In
section

8.2
, we will additionally add boundary variables.
More generally, users of the current system could add shape context constructors at will, as long as they can be interpreted as presheaves over
{\mathcal{W}}
.

4.2.
Mode theory

For simplicity, we take a highly general mode theory and will then only be able to say interesting things about specific modalities and 2-cells.
In practice, and especially in implementations, one will want to select a more syntactic subtheory right away.

As
modes
, we take shape contexts. An interpretation function
\mathopen{}\left\llbracket{\llcorner\hskip-1.72218pt\lrcorner}\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}
from shape contexts to presheaves over
{\mathcal{W}}
will be defined in
section

6.1
.
The mode
{\mathbb{X}}
is modelled in
\mathrm{Psh}({\mathcal{W}}/\mathopen{}\left\llbracket{\mathbb{X}}\right\rrbracket\mathclose{})
.
9
9

9

As we will see later on, the available shapes must in some sense already be present in the base category, so that a context consisting purely of shapes will in general be representable.
As such, we could alternatively interpret modes as
representable
presheaves over
{\mathcal{W}}
, which via the Yoneda-embedding are just the objects of
{\mathcal{W}}
.
This is perfectly possible and would (again by inserting the Yoneda-embedding) require virtually no changes to our approach, although a number of intermediate results in the technical report
[
Nuy23b
]
would become unnecessary.
However, the current approach is strictly more general, allows us to speak about boundaries (definitions
6.8
and
6.24
) in the shape context, and did not require any compromise in the strength of our results.

As
modalities

{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}:{\mathbb{X}}_{1}\to{\mathbb{X}}_{2}
, we take all functors
\mathopen{}\left\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}}\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}:\mathrm{Psh}({\mathcal{W}}/\mathopen{}\left\llbracket{\mathbb{X}}_{2}\right\rrbracket\mathclose{})\to\mathrm{Psh}({\mathcal{W}}/\mathopen{}\left\llbracket{\mathbb{X}}_{1}\right\rrbracket\mathclose{})
which have a right adjoint
\mathopen{}\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}
that is then automatically a weak CwF morphism
[
Nuy23b
]
[
Nuy20
, thm. 6.4.1]
giving rise to a DRA
[
BCM+20
, lemma 17]
[
Nuy18a
]
.
10
10

10

A designated right adjoint can be retrieved from the left adjoint without the axiom of choice
[
Nuy23b
, §2.3.6]
.

As
2-cells

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\alpha}:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\nu}
, we take all natural transformations
\mathopen{}\left\llbracket{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\alpha}}\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}:\mathopen{}\left\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\nu}}\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}\to\mathopen{}\left\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}}\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}
, which automatically give rise to natural transformations
\mathopen{}\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\alpha}\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}:\mathopen{}\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}\to\mathopen{}\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\nu}\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}
.

5.
MTraS Modalities for Substitution

In the previous section, we have defined modalities as left adjoint functors and 2-cells as natural transformation. As such, we have neglected to provide an actual syntax; any syntax we use should be shallowly defined on semantic objects.

We take a similar approach to shape substitutions.
A shape substitution from
{\mathbb{X}}_{1}
to
{\mathbb{X}}_{2}
is defined as a presheaf morphism
\sigma:\mathopen{}\left\llbracket{\mathbb{X}}_{1}\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}\to\mathopen{}\left\llbracket{\mathbb{X}}_{2}\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}
.
We will consistently write the interpretation brackets so as to avoid confusion with modalities
{\mathbb{X}}_{1}\to{\mathbb{X}}_{2}
.
A presheaf morphism
is
not a modality but it gives rise to a pair of modalities:
11
11

11

Note in particular that
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{{\llcorner\hskip-1.72218pt\lrcorner}}]}
turns the arrow around: a presheaf morphism (shape substitution)
\sigma:\mathopen{}\left\llbracket{\mathbb{X}}_{1}\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}\to\mathopen{}\left\llbracket{\mathbb{X}}_{2}\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}
gives rise to a substitution modality
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{\sigma}]}:{\mathbb{X}}_{2}\to{\mathbb{X}}_{1}
sending types
T
in shape context
{\mathbb{X}}_{2}
to types
\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{\sigma}]}\mathrel{|}T\right\rangle\mathclose{}
in shape context
{\mathbb{X}}_{1}
.

Theorem 5.1
.

Any presheaf morphism
\sigma:{\Xi_{1}}\to{\Xi_{2}}
gives rise to a triple of adjoint functors

\Sigma^{\sigma\mathrel{|}}\dashv\Omega^{\sigma\mathrel{|}}\dashv\Pi^{\sigma\mathrel{|}},

\Sigma^{\sigma\mathrel{|}},\Pi^{\sigma\mathrel{|}}:\mathrm{Psh}({\mathcal{W}}/{\Xi_{1}})\to\mathrm{Psh}({\mathcal{W}}/{\Xi_{2}})\qquad\Omega^{\sigma\mathrel{|}}:\mathrm{Psh}({\mathcal{W}}/{\Xi_{2}})\to\mathrm{Psh}({\mathcal{W}}/{\Xi_{1}})

the latter two of which can be internalized as modalities (with an additional left name)
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\Sigma\,{\sigma}}\mathrel{\reflectbox{$\Vdash$}}{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{\sigma}]}\dashv{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Pi\,{\sigma}}
with

\mathopen{}\left\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\Sigma\,{\sigma}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{\sigma}]}}\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}=\Sigma^{\sigma\mathrel{|}},\qquad\mathopen{}\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{\sigma}]}\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}=\Omega^{\sigma\mathrel{|}},\qquad\mathopen{}\left\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\Omega[{\sigma}]}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Pi\,{\sigma}}}\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}=\Omega^{\sigma\mathrel{|}},\qquad\mathopen{}\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Pi\,{\sigma}}\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}=\Pi^{\sigma\mathrel{|}}.

We denote the units and co-units as

\displaystyle\mathsf{copy}^{\sigma\mathrel{|}}

\displaystyle:1\to\Omega^{\sigma\mathrel{|}}\circ\Sigma^{\sigma\mathrel{|}}

\displaystyle\mathsf{drop}^{\sigma\mathrel{|}}

\displaystyle:\Sigma^{\sigma\mathrel{|}}\circ\Omega^{\sigma\mathrel{|}}\to 1

\displaystyle\mathsf{const}^{\sigma\mathrel{|}}

\displaystyle:1\to\Pi^{\sigma\mathrel{|}}\circ\Omega^{\sigma\mathrel{|}}

\displaystyle\mathsf{app}^{\sigma\mathrel{|}}

\displaystyle:\Omega^{\sigma\mathrel{|}}\circ\Pi^{\sigma\mathrel{|}}\to 1

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathsf{drop}_{\sigma}}\mathrel{\reflectbox{$\vDash$}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathsf{const}_{\sigma}}

\displaystyle:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}1}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Pi\,{\sigma}\circ\Omega[{\sigma}]}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathsf{copy}_{\sigma}}\mathrel{\reflectbox{$\vDash$}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathsf{app}_{\sigma}}

\displaystyle:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{\sigma}]\circ\Pi\,{\sigma}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}1}

Under the correspondence of semantic contexts
{\mathbb{X}}\mathrel{|}\Gamma\,\mathsf{ctx}
(i.e. presheaves over
{\mathcal{W}}/\mathopen{}\left\llbracket{\mathbb{X}}\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}
) with semantic types
\mathopen{}\left\llbracket{\mathbb{X}}\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}\vdash\Gamma\,\mathsf{type}
, the functor
\Omega^{\sigma\mathrel{|}}
is exactly the semantics of ordinary type substitution in the standard presheaf model
[
Hof97
]
and hence, if
\sigma
is a weakening substitution, then
\Sigma^{\sigma\mathrel{|}}
and
\Pi^{\sigma\mathrel{|}}
are naturally isomorphic to the semantics of ordinary
\Sigma
- and
\Pi
-types.

The functor
\Omega^{{\llcorner\hskip-1.20552pt\lrcorner}\mathrel{|}}
and the modality
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Pi\,{{\llcorner\hskip-1.72218pt\lrcorner}}}
are strictly functorial (they respect identity and composition of presheaf morphisms on the nose) whereas the functors
\Sigma^{{\llcorner\hskip-1.20552pt\lrcorner}\mathrel{|}}
,
\Pi^{{\llcorner\hskip-1.20552pt\lrcorner}\mathrel{|}}
and the modality
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{{\llcorner\hskip-1.72218pt\lrcorner}}]}
are pseudofunctorial
12
12

12

However, Gratzer et al.
[
GKNB20a
]
have a strictification theorem for models of MTT which could be used to strictify
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{{\llcorner\hskip-1.72218pt\lrcorner}}]}
.
.

Proof 5.2
.

The morphism
\sigma
gives rise to a functor
\Sigma^{/\sigma}:{\mathcal{W}}/\Xi_{1}\to{\mathcal{W}}/\Xi_{2}:(W,\psi)\to(W,\sigma\psi)
and hence via left Kan extension, precomposition and right Kan extension
[
Sta19
]
to a triple of adjoint functors
\Sigma^{\sigma\mathrel{|}}\dashv\Omega^{\sigma\mathrel{|}}\dashv\Pi^{\sigma\mathrel{|}}
between the presheaf categories.
The claim about type substitution follows from unfolding the definitions and the claims about
\Sigma
- and
\Pi
-types then follow from uniqueness of adjoints.

Strict functoriality of
\Omega^{{\llcorner\hskip-1.20552pt\lrcorner}\mathrel{|}}
follows immediately from the construction.
Strict functoriality of
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Pi\,{{\llcorner\hskip-1.72218pt\lrcorner}}}
then follows from the fact that a modality
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}
is fully defined by the semantic left adjoint
\mathopen{}\left\llbracket\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}}\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}
.
Pseudofunctoriality of the others follows by uniqueness of the adjoint.

Remark 5.3

(Substitution as a DRA)
.

In presheaf models, contexts are essentially the same thing as closed types (a property called
democracy
).
The shape substitution operation for contexts
\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\Omega[{\sigma}]}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Pi\,{\sigma}}}
is modelled by
\Omega^{\sigma\mathrel{|}}
, i.e. by ordinary substitution.
However, the shape substitution operation applicable to types is the modal type former
\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{\sigma}]}\mathrel{|}{\llcorner\hskip-1.72218pt\lrcorner}\right\rangle\mathclose{}
, which is
not
equivalent.
Indeed, if
\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{\sigma}]}\mathrel{|}T\right\rangle\mathclose{}
is closed, i.e.
{\mathbb{X}}_{1}\mathrel{|}{\cdot}\vdash\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{\sigma}]}\mathrel{|}T\right\rangle\mathclose{}\,\mathsf{type}
, then
T
lives in context
{\mathbb{X}}_{2}\mathrel{|}{\cdot},\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\Sigma\,{\sigma}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{\sigma}]}}\vdash T\,\mathsf{type}
, so it is not closed.
The operation
\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{\sigma}]}\mathrel{|}{\llcorner\hskip-1.72218pt\lrcorner}\right\rangle\mathclose{}
is in general still modelled as a substitution, but now it is one between the semantic contexts
\mathopen{}\left\llbracket{\mathbb{X}}_{1}\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}.\mathopen{}\left\llbracket\Gamma\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}
and
\mathopen{}\left\llbracket{\mathbb{X}}_{2}\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}.\Sigma^{\sigma\mathrel{|}}\mathopen{}\left\llbracket\Gamma\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}
which are
isomorphic
.
This is especially clear if
\sigma:\mathopen{}\left\llbracket{\mathbb{X}}\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}.\mathopen{}\left\llbracket\Delta\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}\to\mathopen{}\left\llbracket{\mathbb{X}}\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}
is a weakening substitution for a context
{\mathbb{X}}\mathrel{|}\Delta\,\mathsf{ctx}
, in which case we are dealing with
\mathopen{}\left\llbracket{\mathbb{X}}\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}.\mathopen{}\left\llbracket\Delta\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}.\mathopen{}\left\llbracket\Gamma\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}
and
\mathopen{}\left\llbracket{\mathbb{X}}\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}.(\Sigma\mathopen{}\left\llbracket\Delta\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}\mathopen{}\left\llbracket\Gamma\right\rrbracket\mathclose{})
.
We can still let
\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{\sigma}]}\mathrel{|}{\llcorner\hskip-1.72218pt\lrcorner}\right\rangle\mathclose{}
act on a closed type
\Xi_{2}\mathrel{|}{\cdot}\vdash S\,\mathsf{type}
, however, but we first have to weaken
S
to bring it to context
{\cdot},\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\Sigma\,{\sigma}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{\sigma}]}}
.
The composite of these two operations – weakening over
\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\Sigma\,{\sigma}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{\sigma}]}}
and then applying
\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{\sigma}]}\mathrel{|}{\llcorner\hskip-1.72218pt\lrcorner}\right\rangle\mathclose{}
– is in fact equivalent with the operation
\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\Sigma\,{\sigma}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{\sigma}]}}
on contexts.

This remark is relevant to the
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{\sigma}]}
modality specifically because its lock
\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\Sigma\,{\sigma}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{\sigma}]}}
does not preserve the empty context, whereas most other modalities’ locks do.

Notation 1
.

We will use a slightly unconventional notation for
substitutions
in order to have them make maximal sense both as a substitution (as in
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{\sigma}]}
) and as a function domain (as in
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Pi\,{\sigma}}
):

•

Every weakened variable (if weakening is available for the given shape) will be declared, e.g. we get a presheaf morphism
(u:{\mathbb{U}}):\mathopen{}\left\llbracket{\mathbb{X}},u:{\mathbb{U}}\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}\to\mathopen{}\left\llbracket{\mathbb{X}}\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}
and hence
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{u:{\mathbb{U}}}]}:{\mathbb{X}}\to({\mathbb{X}},u:{\mathbb{U}})
and
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Pi({u:{\mathbb{U}}})}:({\mathbb{X}},u:{\mathbb{U}})\to{\mathbb{X}}
.
Furthermore, we may omit the shape, writing just
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{u}]}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Pi\,{u}}
.
Thus, this is what weakening and shape abstraction look like:

\dfrac{\begin{array}[]{l l}{\mathbb{X}}\mathrel{|}\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\Sigma\,{u}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{u}]}}\vdash t:T\end{array}\hfill}{\begin{array}[]{l l}{\mathbb{X}},u:{\mathbb{U}}\mathrel{|}\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{mod}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{u}]}}\,t:\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{u}]}\mathrel{|}T\right\rangle\mathclose{}\end{array}\hfill}\text{},\qquad\dfrac{\begin{array}[]{l l}{\mathbb{X}},u:{\mathbb{U}}\mathrel{|}\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\Omega[{u}]}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Pi\,{u}}}\vdash t:T\end{array}\hfill}{\begin{array}[]{l l}{\mathbb{X}}\mathrel{|}\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{mod}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Pi\,{u}}}\,t:\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Pi\,{u}}\mathrel{|}T\right\rangle\mathclose{}\end{array}\hfill}\text{}.

The projection function (
proposition

3.3
) for
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Pi\,{u}}
is function application:

\displaystyle\dfrac{\begin{array}[]{l l}{\mathbb{X}},u:{\mathbb{U}}\mathrel{|}\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\Omega[{u}]\circ\Sigma\,{u}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{u}]\circ\Pi\,{u}}}\vdash A\,\mathsf{type}\end{array}\hfill}{\begin{array}[]{l l}{\mathbb{X}},u:{\mathbb{U}}\mathrel{|}\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{app}_{u}:({\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{u}]}\shortmid{\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Pi\,{u}}\mathrel{|}A\right\rangle\mathclose{}})\to A\mathopen{}\left[{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathsf{copy}_{u}}:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}1}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\Omega[{u}]\circ\Sigma\,{u}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathsf{app}_{u}}:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{u}]\circ\Pi\,{u}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}1}}\right]\mathclose{}\end{array}\hfill}\text{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:projmod}}

The 2-cell
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\Omega[{u}]\circ\Sigma\,{u}}\Leftarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}1}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathsf{copy}_{u}}\mathrel{\reflectbox{$\vDash$}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathsf{app}_{u}}:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{u}]\circ\Pi\,{u}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}1}
signals a contraction of shape variables, namely of the one bound by the
\Pi
-modality and the one to which the function is applied.

•

When a variable is substituted, we denote this as
u:=t
instead of
t/u
, e.g. in a cubical type theory we get a presheaf morphism
(i:=0):\mathopen{}\left\llbracket{\mathbb{X}}\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}\to\mathopen{}\left\llbracket{\mathbb{X}},i:{\mathbb{I}}\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}
and hence
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{i:=0}]}:({\mathbb{X}},i:{\mathbb{I}})\to{\mathbb{X}}
which binds
i
and
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Pi({i:=0})}:{\mathbb{X}}\to({\mathbb{X}},i:{\mathbb{I}})
which depends on
i
, so we may substitute
0
for
i
but we may also abstract over the assumption that
i
is
0
:

\dfrac{\begin{array}[]{l l}{\mathbb{X}},i:{\mathbb{I}}\mathrel{|}\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\Sigma({i:=0})}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{i:=0}]}}\vdash t:T\end{array}\hfill}{\begin{array}[]{l l}{\mathbb{X}}\mathrel{|}\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{mod}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{i:=0}]}}\,t:\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{i:=0}]}\mathrel{|}T\right\rangle\mathclose{}\end{array}\hfill}\text{},\qquad\dfrac{\begin{array}[]{l l}{\mathbb{X}}\mathrel{|}\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\Omega[{i:=0}]}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Pi({i:=0})}}\vdash t:T\end{array}\hfill}{\begin{array}[]{l l}{\mathbb{X}},i:{\mathbb{I}}\mathrel{|}\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{mod}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Pi({i:=0})}}\,t:\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Pi({i:=0})}\mathrel{|}T\right\rangle\mathclose{}\end{array}\hfill}\text{}.

And apply:

\displaystyle\dfrac{\begin{array}[]{l l}{\mathbb{X}}\mathrel{|}\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\Omega[{i:=0}]\circ\Sigma({i:=0})}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{i:=0}]\circ\Pi({i:=0})}}\vdash A\,\mathsf{type}\end{array}\hfill}{\begin{array}[]{l l}{\mathbb{X}}\mathrel{|}\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{app}_{i:=0}:({\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{i:=0}]}\shortmid{\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Pi({i:=0})}\mathrel{|}A\right\rangle\mathclose{}})\to A\mathopen{}\left[{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathsf{copy}_{i:=0}}:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}1}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\Omega[{i:=0}]\circ\Sigma({i:=0})}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathsf{app}_{i:=0}}:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{i:=0}]\circ\Pi({i:=0})}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}1}}\right]\mathclose{}\end{array}\hfill}\text{prop.\ \ref{thm:projmod}}

•

Finally, if
\sigma
involves weakening, then the codomain of the co-unit may be a
variable renaming
that is sugar for the identity, e.g.

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathsf{app}_{(u/v:{\mathbb{U}})}}:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{u:{\mathbb{U}}}]\circ\Pi({v:{\mathbb{U}}})}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{u:{\mathbb{U}},v:=u}]}

is exactly the same thing as

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathsf{app}_{(u:{\mathbb{U}})}}:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{u:{\mathbb{U}}}]\circ\Pi({u:{\mathbb{U}}})}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}1}.

This way, we may adjust
\mathsf{app}_{(u:{\mathbb{U}})}
in order to be able to apply to a
different
variable:

\displaystyle\dfrac{\begin{array}[]{l l}{\mathbb{X}},v:{\mathbb{U}}\mathrel{|}\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\Omega[{v}]\circ\Sigma\,{u}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{u}]\circ\Pi\,{v}}}\vdash A\,\mathsf{type}\end{array}\hfill}{\begin{array}[]{l l}{\mathbb{X}},u:{\mathbb{U}}\mathrel{|}\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{app}_{u/v}:({\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{u}]}\shortmid{\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Pi\,{v}}\mathrel{|}A\right\rangle\mathclose{}})\to\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{u,v:=u}]}\mathrel{|}A\mathopen{}\left[{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathsf{copy}_{v/u}}:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\Omega[{v,u:=v}]}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\Omega[{u}]\circ\Sigma\,{u}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathsf{app}_{u/v}}:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{u}]\circ\Pi\,{u}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{u,v:=u}]}}\right]\mathclose{}\right\rangle\mathclose{}\end{array}\hfill}\text{}

Again, bear in mind that shape substitutions are in fact defined as presheaf morphisms and that therefore, notions such as
weakening
and
contraction
reflected by the syntax introduced here, need to be shallowly interpreted in presheaf morphisms.

Example 5.4
.

If
{\mathbb{U}}
is cartesian, i.e.
\mathopen{}\left\llbracket{\mathbb{X}},u:{\mathbb{U}}\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}=\mathopen{}\left\llbracket{\mathbb{X}}\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}\times\mathopen{}\left\llbracket{\mathbb{U}}\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}
, then there is a diagonal substitution
(w:{\mathbb{U}},u:=w,v:=w):\mathopen{}\left\llbracket{\mathbb{X}},w:{\mathbb{U}}\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}\to\mathopen{}\left\llbracket{\mathbb{X}},u:{\mathbb{U}},v:{\mathbb{U}}\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}
.
Writing

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\alpha}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}1_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Pi\,{u}}}\mathbin{\star}1_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Pi\,{v}}}\mathbin{\star}\mathsf{const}_{(w,u:=w,v:=w)}}:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Pi\,{u}\circ\Pi\,{v}}\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\qquad{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Pi\,{u}\circ\Pi\,{v}\circ\Pi({w,u:=w,v:=w})\circ\Omega[{w,u:=w,v:=w}]}

\displaystyle\qquad={\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Pi({(u:{\mathbb{U}}})\circ(v:{\mathbb{U}})\circ(w,u:=w,v:=w))\circ\Omega[{w,u:=w,v:=w}]}

\displaystyle\qquad={\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Pi({(u:{\mathbb{U}}})\circ(w,u:=w))\circ\Omega[{w,u:=w,v:=w}]}

\displaystyle\qquad={\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Pi\,{w}\circ\Omega[{w,u:=w,v:=w}]},

where the equations use strict functoriality of
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Pi\,{{\llcorner\hskip-1.72218pt\lrcorner}}}
and ordinary calculation of composition of substitutions,
this allows us to type the naïvely typed function
\lambda f.\lambda w.f\,w\,w:(\Pi\,{u}.\Pi\,{v}.A)\to\Pi\,{w}.A[w/u,w/v]
as

\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Pi({u:{\mathbb{U}}})}\mathrel{|}\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Pi({v:{\mathbb{U}}})}\mathrel{|}A\right\rangle\mathclose{}\right\rangle\mathclose{}\to\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Pi({w:{\mathbb{U}}})}\mathrel{|}\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{w:{\mathbb{U}},u:=w,v:=w}]}\mathrel{|}A[{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\alpha}}]\right\rangle\mathclose{}\right\rangle\mathclose{}.

Remark 5.5
.

The reframing of shape substitutions as a modality, has the annoying consequence that substitution no longer reduces.
However, both
\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{\sigma}]}\mathrel{|}{\llcorner\hskip-1.72218pt\lrcorner}\right\rangle\mathclose{}
and
\mathsf{mod}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{\sigma}]}}
are semantically an ordinary substitution (along an isomorphism, see
remark

5.3
).
Thus, we could add computation rules such as:

\begin{array}[]{r c l r c l}\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{\sigma}]}\mathrel{|}A\times B\right\rangle\mathclose{}&=&\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{\sigma}]}\mathrel{|}A\right\rangle\mathclose{}\times\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{\sigma}]}\mathrel{|}B\right\rangle\mathclose{},&{}\quad\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{\sigma}]}\mathrel{|}\mathsf{U}\right\rangle\mathclose{}&=&\mathsf{U},\\
\mathsf{mod}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{\sigma}]}}\,(a,b)&=&(\mathsf{mod}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{\sigma}]}}\,a,\mathsf{mod}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{\sigma}]}}\,b),&\mathsf{mod}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{\sigma}]}}\,\ulcorner A\urcorner&=&\ulcorner\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{\sigma}]}\mathrel{|}A\right\rangle\mathclose{}\urcorner.\end{array}

This is fine in an extensional type system, but would not play well with the
\beta
-rule for modal types in an intensional system.
Indeed,
\beta
-reduction for
\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{\sigma}]}\mathrel{|}A\right\rangle\mathclose{}
requires a solution to the following problem: when
\hat{a}=\mathsf{mod}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{\sigma}]}}\,a
definitionally, then we need to be able to infer
a
up to definitional equality from
\hat{a}
.
Alternatively, the eliminator for
\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{\sigma}]}\mathrel{|}A\right\rangle\mathclose{}
should somehow proceed by induction on
A
, e.g. an element of
\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{\sigma}]}\mathrel{|}A\times B\right\rangle\mathclose{}
could be eliminated as an element of
\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{\sigma}]}\mathrel{|}A\right\rangle\mathclose{}\times\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{\sigma}]}\mathrel{|}B\right\rangle\mathclose{}
.
A third possibility would be to abolish elimination of
\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{\sigma}]}\mathrel{|}{\llcorner\hskip-1.72218pt\lrcorner}\right\rangle\mathclose{}
altogether, except when applied to type formers for which there is no definitional substitution-commutation law.

Remark 5.6
.

In type theory, we generally expect admissibility of substitution: given a derivable judgement
\Gamma\vdash J
and a substitution
\sigma:\Delta\to\Gamma
, we expect derivability of
\Delta\vdash J[\sigma]
, where the operation
{\llcorner\hskip-1.72218pt\lrcorner}[\sigma]
can be applied to any term, type or other object in context and traverses its structure, leaving everything untouched except variables.
A good way to guarantee admissibility of substitution is by making sure that every inference rule has a conclusion in a general context
\Gamma
and that the context of any premise is obtained by applying a functorial operation to
\Gamma
.

There is no such result for shape substitutions.

The conclusion of modal inference rules often has a non-general shape context, and the transpension type is in general not even respected by shape substitution
[
Nuy23b
]
.
However, until we extend MTraS with additional rules in
sections

8
,
9
and
10
, we do have a form of the usual result: given a derivable judgement
{\mathbb{X}}\mathrel{|}\Gamma\vdash J
and a substitution
{\mathbb{X}}\mathrel{|}\sigma:\Delta\to\Gamma
, we can derive
{\mathbb{X}}\mathrel{|}\Delta\vdash J[\sigma]
.

6.
Multipliers

In this section, we introduce multipliers as a semantics for shapes, as well as the associated modalities
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Finv[{u}]}\dashv{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\forall\,{u}}\dashv{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}{\between}[{u}]}
.
In
section

6.1
, we define multipliers and a number of criteria by which we can classify them.
In
section

6.2
, we deal with a technical complication that we dubbed
unpointability
§
A
, which shows up especially in models of guarded and nominal type theory.
In
section

6.3
, we discuss an extensive number of examples.
In
section

6.4
, we consider how
copointed
§
A
multipliers give rise to
shape weakening
modalities that are a special instance of the modalities in
section

5
.
In
section

6.5
, we discuss how a multiplier and its associated operations lift from acting on base and slice categories to acting on categories of elements of semantic shape contexts.
Then in
section

6.6
, we are finally ready to define the transpension modality and its adjoints and to state the quantification
theorem

6.33
that helps to understand them.
In
section

6.7
, we say a bit more on cartesian multipliers.
In
section

6.8
, we briefly list the matters that are not discussed in the current paper but can be found in the technical report
[
Nuy23b
]
.

6.1.
Shapes and multipliers

In
section

4
, we defined shape contexts as lists of variables and announced that these would be modelled as presheaves over
{\mathcal{W}}
.
Several times, we have hinted at the fact that these shape variables need not satisfy all the usual structural rules (weakening, exchange and contraction).
In this section, we make these matters precise.

We associate to each shape
{\mathbb{U}}
a functor
{\llcorner\hskip-1.72218pt\lrcorner}\ltimes U:{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{W}}
which extends by left Kan extension to a functor
{\llcorner\hskip-1.72218pt\lrcorner}\ltimes\mathbf{y}U:\mathrm{Psh}({\mathcal{W}})\to\mathrm{Psh}({\mathcal{W}})
.
13
13

13

Both
{\llcorner\hskip-1.72218pt\lrcorner}\ltimes U
and
{\llcorner\hskip-1.72218pt\lrcorner}\ltimes\mathbf{y}U
are to be regarded as single-character symbols, i.e.
\ltimes
in itself is meaningless.
In most concrete applications, however, the multiplier is defined as some monoidal product
{\llcorner\hskip-1.72218pt\lrcorner}\otimes U
with a given object
U
, in which case the left Kan extension is naturally isomorphic to Day convolution with
\mathbf{y}U
.
For this reason, we also refrain from defining
U:=\top\ltimes U
because we may not have
\top\otimes U=U
on the nose for the object of interest
U
.

We define the semantics of shape contexts
\mathopen{}\left\llbracket{\llcorner\hskip-1.72218pt\lrcorner}\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}:\mathrm{Shp}\mathrm{Ctx}\to\mathrm{Obj}\mathopen{}\left(\mathrm{Psh}({\mathcal{W}})\right)\mathclose{}
as follows:

\mathopen{}\left\llbracket\cdot\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}=\top,\qquad\mathopen{}\left\llbracket{\mathbb{X}},u:{\mathbb{U}}\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}=\mathopen{}\left\llbracket{\mathbb{X}}\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}\ltimes\mathbf{y}U.

Of course, if we model shape context extension with
u:{\mathbb{U}}
by an
arbitrary
functor, then we will not be able to prove many results.
Depending on the properties of the functor, the variable
u
will obey different structural rules and the
\Phi
-combinator
[
Mou16
,
BCM15
]
will or will not be sound for
{\mathbb{U}}
.
For this reason, we introduce some criteria that help us classify shapes.
Some of these criteria concern in fact the
fresh weakening functor
for the given multiplier, which is essentially an instance of the following construction:

Definition 6.1
.

Given a functor
F:{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{W}}
and
V_{0}\in\mathrm{Obj}\mathopen{}\left({\mathcal{V}}\right)\mathclose{}
, we define the action of
F
on slice objects over
V_{0}
as the functor

F^{/V_{0}}:{\mathcal{V}}/V_{0}\to{\mathcal{W}}/FV_{0}:(V,\varphi)\mapsto(FV,F\varphi).

Definition 6.2
.

Assume
{\mathcal{W}}
has a terminal object
\top
. A
multiplier
for an object
U
is an endofunctor
14
14

14

In the technical report
[
Nuy23b
]
, we generalize beyond endofunctors.

{\llcorner\hskip-1.72218pt\lrcorner}\ltimes U:{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{W}}
such that
\top\ltimes U\cong U
.
This gives us a natural second projection
\pi_{2}:({\llcorner\hskip-1.72218pt\lrcorner}\ltimes U)\to U
.

We define the
fresh weakening functor
to the slice category as
\Finv_{U}:{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{W}}/U:W\mapsto(W\ltimes U,\pi_{2})
, which is essentially the action of the multiplier on slice objects over
\top
.

We say that a multiplier (as well as its shape) is:

•

Copointed
§
A
if it is equipped with a natural first projection
\pi_{1}:({\llcorner\hskip-1.72218pt\lrcorner}\ltimes U)\to\mathrm{Id}
.

This property carries over to
{\llcorner\hskip-1.72218pt\lrcorner}\ltimes\mathbf{y}U
and thus allows for shape weakening.

•

A
comonad
§
A
if it is additionally equipped with a natural diagonal
\delta:({\llcorner\hskip-1.72218pt\lrcorner}\ltimes U)\to(({\llcorner\hskip-1.72218pt\lrcorner}\ltimes U)\ltimes U)
such that
\pi_{1}\circ\delta=(\pi_{1}\ltimes U)\circ\delta=1_{({\llcorner\hskip-1.20552pt\lrcorner}\ltimes U)}
and
\delta\circ\delta=(\delta\ltimes U)\circ\delta:({\llcorner\hskip-1.72218pt\lrcorner}\ltimes U)\to({\llcorner\hskip-1.72218pt\lrcorner}\ltimes U)^{3}
.

This property carries over to
{\llcorner\hskip-1.72218pt\lrcorner}\ltimes\mathbf{y}U
and thus allows for shape variable contraction.

•

Cartesian
if it is naturally isomorphic to the cartesian product with
U
.

This property carries over to
{\llcorner\hskip-1.72218pt\lrcorner}\ltimes\mathbf{y}U
and is thus a sufficient condition for allowing exchange. Additionally, it will erase the distinction between
weakening

{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{u}]}
(
section

6.4
) and
fresh shape weakening

{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Finv[{u}]}
(
section

6.6
), see
theorem

6.33
.

•

\top
-slice faithful
§
A
if
\Finv_{U}
is faithful.

This is a basic well-behavedness property that is satisfied by all examples of interest.
Example

6.22
is a counterexample.

•

\top
-slice full
§
A
if
\Finv_{U}
is full.

For multipliers over objects other than
\top
, this property precludes the exchange rule (
proposition

6.3
).

\top
-slice fully faithful multipliers will give rise to fully faithful modalities for fresh weakening
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Finv[{u}]}
and transpension
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}{\between}[{u}]}
(
theorem

6.33
).

•

\top
-slice shard-free
§
A
if
\Finv_{U}
is essentially surjective on slice objects
(V,\psi)
such that
\psi:V\to U
is dimensionally split (
definition

6.8
). A
shard
§
A
is a slice object
(V,\psi)
that is not up to isomorphism in the image of
\Finv_{U}
even though
\psi
is dimensionally split.

Intuitively, a shard is a shape over
{\mathbb{U}}
that covers all of
{\mathbb{U}}
(as expressed by the fact that
\psi
is dimensionally split, which just means split epi in most applications), but that is not prism-shaped in the direction of
{\mathbb{U}}
(as it would then be in the image of
\Finv_{U}
).
Shard-freedom will be a requirement for the
\Phi
-rule
[
Mou16
]
to hold (
theorem

10.1
) and for elimination of the transpension type by pattern matching to be sound (
theorem

9.4
).

•

\top
-slice right adjoint
if
\Finv_{U}
has a left adjoint
\exists_{U}:{\mathcal{W}}/U\to{\mathcal{W}}
.
15
15

15

A functor
{\llcorner\hskip-1.72218pt\lrcorner}\ltimes U
with this property is usually called a
parametric
or
local right adjoint
, but the word ‘local’ is overloaded
[
nLa23b
]
and so is ‘parametric’, and we wanted uniform terminology.

Proposition 6.3
.

If a
\top
-slice full multiplier for
U
is:

•

a comonad, then
U
is terminal,

•

cartesian, then it is naturally isomorphic to the identity functor.

Proof 6.4
.

The second statement clearly follows from the first, so we only prove the first.
Consider the following diagram:

\textstyle{\top\ltimes U\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{2}}
\textstyle{(\top\ltimes U)\ltimes U\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{2}}
\textstyle{U}

(2)

It commutes, because
\pi_{2}=\pi_{2}\circ(\pi_{1}\ltimes U):(\top\ltimes U)\ltimes U
and
(\pi_{1}\ltimes U)\circ\delta=1
.
So it is a morphism of slice objects
\delta:\Finv_{U}\top\to\Finv_{U}(\top\times U)
and thus, since
\Finv_{U}
is full, of the form
\Finv_{U}\upsilon
for some
\upsilon:\top\to\top\times U
. This means in particular that

{\mathrm{id}}_{\top\times U}=\pi_{1}\circ\delta=\pi_{1}\circ(\upsilon\times U)=\upsilon\circ\pi_{1}:\top\times U\to\top\times U,

(3)

so the identity on
\top\ltimes U
factors over
\top
. Then
\top\ltimes U\cong U
is terminal.

6.2.
Pointability, dimensional splitness and boundaries

Before we move on to a list of examples, we owe the reader a definition for dimensional splitness (although the impatient reader may first read the example
section

6.3
, ignoring shard-freedom, pointability and boundaries).
In most popular base categories, namely all the
objectwise pointable
ones, we could have gotten away with saying ‘split epi’ instead of ‘dimensionally split’ (
proposition

6.10
).

Definition 6.5
.

Let
{\mathcal{W}}
be a category with terminal object
\top
. An object
W
is
pointable
§
A
if
():W\to\top
is split epi, i.e. if there exists at least one morphism
\top\to W
.
A category is
objectwise pointable
§
A
if each object is pointable.

We have carefully chosen the above terminology to emphasize (1) that pointability is a property, not structure (the corresponding structure is called
pointed
), and (2) that objectwise pointability does
not
require that the pointings can be chosen naturally.

Proposition 6.6
.

Let
{\llcorner\hskip-1.72218pt\lrcorner}\ltimes U
be a multiplier on an objectwise pointable category
{\mathcal{W}}
. Then for any object
W
, the slice object
\Finv_{U}W
is split epi.

Proof 6.7
.

Any functor preserves split epimorphisms. We have
\Finv_{U}W=(W\ltimes U,\pi_{2})
and
\pi_{2}:W\ltimes U\to U\cong\top\ltimes U
is essentially the image of
W\to\top
.

When dealing with a category that is not objectwise pointable, the above theorem does not hold and the definition of shard-freedom w.r.t. split epi slice objects would not make sense, so we need a somewhat more general notion:

Definition 6.8
.

Given a multiplier
{\llcorner\hskip-1.72218pt\lrcorner}\ltimes U:{\mathcal{W}}\to{\mathcal{W}}
, we say that a morphism
\varphi:V\to U
is
dimensionally split
if there is some
W\in{\mathcal{W}}
such that
\pi_{2}:W\ltimes U\to U
factors over
\varphi
. The other factor
\chi:W\ltimes U\to V
such that
\pi_{2}=\varphi\circ\chi
will be called a
(dimensional) section
of
\varphi
. We write
{\mathcal{W}}/\hskip-2.15277pt/U
for the full subcategory of
{\mathcal{W}}/U
of dimensionally split slice objects.

We define the
boundary

\partial U
as the subpresheaf of the Yoneda-embedding
\mathbf{y}U
consisting of those morphisms that are
not
dimensionally split.

Thus, a multiplier is
\top
-slice shard-free if and only if every dimensionally split slice has an invertible dimensional section.

Proposition 6.9
.

Let
{\llcorner\hskip-1.72218pt\lrcorner}\ltimes U
be a multiplier on
{\mathcal{W}}
. Then for any object
W
, the slice
\Finv_{U}W
is dimensionally split with section
{\mathrm{id}}_{W\ltimes U}
. ∎

Proposition 6.10
.

If
{\mathcal{W}}
is objectwise pointable, then a morphism
\varphi:V\to U
is split epi if and only if it is dimensionally split.
[
Nuy23b
]

The notion of dimensionally split morphisms lets us consider the boundary and shard-freedom (a requirement for modelling
\Phi
) also in base categories that are not objectwise pointable, where the output of
\Finv_{U}
may not be split epi.

Remark 6.11
.

\top
-slice shard-freedom can also be formulated using (co)sieves
[
nLa23d
]
.
A
sieve in
{\mathcal{W}}
is a full subcategory
\mathcal{S}
such that if
V\in\mathcal{S}
and
\varphi:V\to W
, then
W\in\mathcal{S}
.
The dual (where
\varphi
points the other way) is called a
cosieve in
{\mathcal{W}}
.
Being full subcategories, (co)sieves can be regarded as subsets of
\mathrm{Obj}\mathopen{}\left({\mathcal{W}}\right)\mathclose{}
.
A
sieve on
U\in{\mathcal{W}}
is a sieve in
{\mathcal{W}}/U
or, equivalently, a subpresheaf of
\mathbf{y}U
.

A multiplier is
\top
-slice shard-free if either of the following equivalent criteria is satisfied:

•

The objects in the essential image of
\Finv_{U}
constitute a cosieve in
{\mathcal{W}}/U

[
Nuy
]
.

•

The objects
outside
the essential image of
\Finv_{U}
constitute a sieve in
{\mathcal{W}}/U
, i.e. a sieve on
U
.

The slice objects of the cosieve generated by objects of the essential image of
\Finv_{U}
, are called dimensionally split.
The boundary
\partial U
is the largest sieve on
U
that is disjoint with the objects of the essential image of
\Finv_{U}
.

6.3.
Examples

Let us look at some examples of multipliers.
Their properties are listed in
fig.

7
.
Most properties are easy to verify, so we omit the proofs.

Example 6.12

(Identity)
.

The identity functor on an arbitrary category
{\mathcal{W}}
is a multiplier for
\top
.
All slice objects
(W,())
are dimensionally split with invertible section
{\mathrm{id}}_{W}:\mathrm{Id}\,W\cong W
; hence the boundary is empty and there are no shards.
It is
\top
-slice right adjoint, with
\exists_{\top}:{\mathcal{W}}/\top\to{\mathcal{W}}:(W,())\mapsto W
.

Example 6.13

(Cartesian product)
.

Let
{\mathcal{W}}
be a category with finite products and
U\in{\mathcal{W}}
.
Then
{\llcorner\hskip-1.72218pt\lrcorner}\times U
is a multiplier for
U
, which is
\top
-slice full if and only if it is the identity (i.e.
U
is terminal, cf.
propositions

6.3
and
6.12
).
It is
\top
-slice right adjoint with
\exists_{U}:{\mathcal{W}}/U\to{\mathcal{W}}:(W,\psi)\mapsto W
.
Hence, we have
\exists_{U}\Finv_{U}={\llcorner\hskip-1.72218pt\lrcorner}\times U
.

.

Let
{\mathsf{RG}}
be the category generated by the following diagram and equations:

is a reflexive graph.

Example 6.15

(Cartesian cubes)
.

Let
{}^{a}{{\mathsf{Cube}}}
be the category of cartesian
a
-ary cubes.
It is the free cartesian monoidal category with same terminal object over
{}^{a}{{\mathsf{RG}}}
. Concretely:

•

Its objects take the form
(i_{1}:{\mathbb{I}},\ldots,i_{n}:{\mathbb{I}})
(the names are desugared to de Bruijn indices, i.e. the objects are really just natural numbers),

•

Its morphisms
(i_{1}:{\mathbb{I}},\ldots,i_{n}:{\mathbb{I}})\to(j_{1}:{\mathbb{I}},\ldots,j_{m}:{\mathbb{I}})
are arbitrary functions

\varphi:\mathopen{}\left\{j_{1},\ldots,j_{m}\right\}\mathclose{}\to\mathopen{}\left\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}\right\}\mathclose{}\cup\mathopen{}\left\{0,\ldots,a-1\right\}\mathclose{}:j\mapsto j\langle\varphi\rangle.

We also write
\varphi=(j_{1}\langle\varphi\rangle/j_{1},\ldots,j_{m}\langle\varphi\rangle/j_{m})
. If a variable
i
is not used, we may write
i/\oslash
to emphasize this.

This category is objectwise pointable if and only if
a\neq 0
.
On this category, we consider the multiplier
{\llcorner\hskip-1.72218pt\lrcorner}\times(i:{\mathbb{I}})
, which is an instance of
example

6.13
and therefore inherits all properties of cartesian multipliers.
A slice
(V,\varphi)
where
\varphi:V\to(i:{\mathbb{I}})
is dimensionally split if
i\langle\varphi\rangle
is not an endpoint, i.e.
i\langle\varphi\rangle\not\in\mathopen{}\left\{0,\ldots,a-1\right\}\mathclose{}
, and in that case it is isomorphic to
\Finv_{(i:{\mathbb{I}})}V^{\prime}
where
V^{\prime}
is
V
with
i\langle\varphi\rangle
removed, so there are no shards.
Clearly then, any morphism on the boundary factors through one of
a
morphisms
(\varepsilon/i):\top\to(i:{\mathbb{I}})
where
\varepsilon\in\mathopen{}\left\{0,\ldots,a-1\right\}\mathclose{}
, so
\partial{\mathbb{I}}\cong\biguplus_{k=0}^{a-1}\top
.

Example 6.16

(Affine cubes)
.

Let
{}^{a}{{\mathsf{Cube}}}_{\Box}
be the category of affine
a
-ary cubes as used in
[
BCH14
]
(binary) or
[
BCM15
]
(unary).
It is the free semicartesian monoidal category with same terminal unit over
{}^{a}{{\mathsf{RG}}}
. Concretely:

•

Objects are as in
{}^{a}{{\mathsf{Cube}}}
,

•

Morphisms are as in
{}^{a}{{\mathsf{Cube}}}
such that if
j\langle\varphi\rangle=k\langle\varphi\rangle\not\in\mathopen{}\left\{0,\ldots,a-1\right\}\mathclose{}
, then
j=k
. This rules out diagonal maps.

This category is objectwise pointable if and only if
a\neq 0
.
On this category, we consider the functor
{\llcorner\hskip-1.72218pt\lrcorner}*(i:{\mathbb{I}}):W\mapsto(W,i:{\mathbb{I}})
, which is a multiplier for
(i:{\mathbb{I}})
.
Dimensional splitness and the boundary are as in
{}^{a}{{\mathsf{Cube}}}
.
This functor is
\top
-slice right adjoint with
\exists_{(i:{\mathbb{I}})}((W,j:{\mathbb{I}}),(j/i))=W
and
\exists_{(i:{\mathbb{I}})}(W,(\varepsilon/i))=W
for each of the
a
endpoints
\varepsilon
.

In the nullary case,
{}^{0}{{\mathsf{Cube}}}_{\Box}
is the base category of the Schanuel topos, a sheaf topos equivalent to the category of nominal sets
[
Pit13a
]
.
In that case,
\exists_{(i:{\mathbb{I}})}
is not just left adjoint to
\Finv_{(i:{\mathbb{I}})}
, but in fact an inverse and hence also right adjoint.
This is in line with the fact that in nominal type theory
[
PMD15
]
, there is a single name quantifier which can be read as either existential or universal quantification.
