Recovering known operators: Dependency graph

10.1.
The amazing right adjoint
\surd

Licata et al.
[
LOPS18
]
use presheaves over a cartesian base category of cubes and introduce
\surd
as the right adjoint to the non-dependent exponential
{\mathbb{I}}\to{\llcorner\hskip-1.72218pt\lrcorner}
.
We generalize to
semicartesian
base categories (indeed to copointed multipliers) and look for a right adjoint to
{\mathbb{U}}\multimap{\llcorner\hskip-1.72218pt\lrcorner}
, which decomposes as substructural quantification after cartesian weakening
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\forall({u:{\mathbb{U}}})\circ\Omega[{u:{\mathbb{U}}}]}
.
Then the right adjoint is obviously
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mathbin{\surd}_{\mathbb{U}}}:={\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Pi({u:{\mathbb{U}}})\circ{\between}[{u:{\mathbb{U}}}]}
.
The type constructor has type
\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mathbin{\surd}_{\mathbb{U}}}\mathrel{|}{\llcorner\hskip-1.72218pt\lrcorner}\right\rangle\mathclose{}:({\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mathbin{\surd}_{\mathbb{U}}}\shortmid{\mathsf{U}_{\ell}})\to\mathsf{U}_{\ell}
and the transposition rule is as in
proposition

3.4
.
This is an improvement in two ways: First, we have introduction, elimination and computation rules, so that we do not need to postulate functoriality of
\mathbin{\surd}_{\mathbb{U}}
and invertibility of transposition.
Secondly, we have no need for a global sections modality
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\flat}
.
Instead, we use the modality
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mathbin{\surd}_{\mathbb{U}}}
to escape Licata et al.’s no-go theorems.

Our overly general mode theory does contain a global sections modality
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\flat}:\cdot\to\cdot
acting in the empty shape context, and we can use this to recover Licata et al.’s axioms for the amazing right adjoint.
Let us write
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}1}\Leftarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\Sigma\,{u}\circ\Finv[{u}]}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathsf{spdrop}_{u}}\mathrel{\reflectbox{$\vDash$}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathsf{spconst}_{u}}:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}1}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\forall\,{u}\circ\Omega[{u}]}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathsf{spunmer}_{u}}:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Pi\,{u}\circ{\between}[{u}]}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}1}
for the 2-cells built by partial transposition from either
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathsf{hide}_{u}}\mathrel{\reflectbox{$\vDash$}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathsf{spoil}_{u}}
or
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathsf{cospoil}_{u}}
(
theorem

6.33
), which are each other’s transposite. The following are isomorphisms:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\kappa}:={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathsf{spconst}_{u}\mathbin{\star}1_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\flat}}}

\displaystyle:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\flat}\cong{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\forall\,{u}\circ\Omega[{u}]\circ\flat},

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\zeta}:={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}1_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\flat}}\mathbin{\star}\mathsf{spunmer}_{u}}

\displaystyle:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\flat\circ\Pi\,{u}\circ{\between}[{u}]}\cong{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\flat}.

For
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\kappa}
, this is intuitively clear from the fact that we are considering
{\mathbb{U}}
-cells in a discrete presheaf produced by
\flat
. For
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\zeta}
, this is similarly clear after taking the left adjoints:

{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathsf{spconst}_{u}\mathbin{\star}1_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\rbag}}}:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\rbag}\cong{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\forall\,{u}\circ\Omega[{u}]\circ\rbag}

where
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\rbag}
, the left name of
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\flat}
, is semantically the connected components functor which also produces discrete presheaves.
Write
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\rbag}\Leftarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}1}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\eta}\mathrel{\reflectbox{$\vDash$}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\varepsilon}:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\flat}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}1}
for the co-unit of the comonad
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\flat}
.
We can define

\displaystyle{\mathbb{U}}\mathbin{\surd}{\llcorner\hskip-1.72218pt\lrcorner}:({\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\flat}\shortmid{\mathsf{U}})\to\mathsf{U}

\displaystyle{\mathbb{U}}\multimap{\llcorner\hskip-1.72218pt\lrcorner}:\mathsf{U}\to\mathsf{U}

\displaystyle{\mathbb{U}}\mathbin{\surd}A=\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Pi\,{u}\circ{\between}[{u}]}\mathrel{|}A[{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\zeta^{-1}}}][{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\eta}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\varepsilon}},\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\forall\,{u}\circ\Omega[{u}]}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Pi\,{u}\circ{\between}[{u}]}}]\right\rangle\mathclose{}

\displaystyle{\mathbb{U}}\multimap A=\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\forall\,{u}\circ\Omega[{u}]}\mathrel{|}A[{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathsf{spdrop}_{u}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathsf{spconst}_{u}}}]\right\rangle\mathclose{}.

Let two global types
\top\mathrel{|}\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\flat}}\vdash A,B\,\mathsf{type}
be given.
Applying the non-dependent version of
proposition

3.4
to the adjunction
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\forall\,{u}\circ\Omega[{u}]}\dashv{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mathbin{\surd}_{\mathbb{U}}}={\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Pi\,{u}\circ{\between}[{u}]}
with unit
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}(1_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Pi\,{u}}}\mathbin{\star}\mathsf{reidx}_{u}\mathbin{\star}1_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{u}]}})\circ\mathsf{const}_{u}}:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}1}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Pi\,{u}\circ{\between}[{u}]\circ\forall\,{u}\circ\Omega[{u}]}
yields:

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\left(A[{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\eta}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\varepsilon}}]\to{\mathbb{U}}\mathbin{\surd}B\right)\mathclose{}

\displaystyle\cong\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mathbin{\surd}_{\mathbb{U}}}\mathrel{|}({\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\forall\,{u}\circ\Omega[{u}]}\shortmid{A[{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\eta}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\varepsilon}}][{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathsf{drop}_{u}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathsf{const}_{u}}}][\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\Omega[{u}]}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Pi\,{u}}},{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathsf{app}_{u}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathsf{reidx}_{u}}},\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\Sigma\,{u}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{u}]}}]})\to B[{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\zeta^{-1}}}][{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\eta}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\varepsilon}},\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\forall\,{u}\circ\Omega[{u}]}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Pi\,{u}\circ{\between}[{u}]}}]\right\rangle\mathclose{}

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mathbin{\surd}_{\mathbb{U}}}\mathrel{|}({\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\forall\,{u}\circ\Omega[{u}]}\shortmid{A[{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathsf{spdrop}_{u}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathsf{spconst}_{u}}}]})[{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\zeta^{-1}}}][{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\eta}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\varepsilon}},\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\forall\,{u}\circ\Omega[{u}]}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Pi\,{u}\circ{\between}[{u}]}}]\to B[{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\zeta^{-1}}}][{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\eta}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\varepsilon}},\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\forall\,{u}\circ\Omega[{u}]}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Pi\,{u}\circ{\between}[{u}]}}]\right\rangle\mathclose{}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{U}}\mathbin{\surd}\mathopen{}\left(({\mathbb{U}}\multimap A)\to B\right)\mathclose{}.

Equality of the substitutions applied to
A
is proven by transposing
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\forall\,{u}\circ\Omega[{u}]}
to the left as
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Pi\,{u}\circ{\between}[{u}]}
. Then the unit
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathsf{const}_{u}\circ(1_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Pi\,{u}}}\mathbin{\star}\mathsf{reidx}_{u}\mathbin{\star}1_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Omega[{u}]}})}:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}1}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Pi\,{u}\circ{\between}[{u}]\circ\forall\,{u}\circ\Omega[{u}]}
becomes
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}1_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Pi\,{u}\circ{\between}[{u}]}}}
, leaving just
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\varepsilon}:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\flat}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}1}
, whereas
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathsf{spconst}_{u}}:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}1}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\forall\,{u}\circ\Omega[{u}]}
becomes
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathsf{spunmer}_{u}}:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Pi\,{u}\circ{\between}[{u}]}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}1}
and cancels against
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\zeta^{-1}}
, again leaving just
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\varepsilon}
.

Applying the
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\flat}
modality to both sides of the isomorphism and using
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\zeta}
to get rid of the amazing right adjoint on the right, yields transposition functions as given by Licata et al.
[
LOPS18
]
.

We refer back to
section

6.7
for the opposite construction: a transpension type for a cartesian multiplier can be constructed from an amazing right adjoint
[
Yet87
]
.

10.2.
The
\Phi
-combinator

Binary and destrictified reformulation of the original
\Phi
-rule
[
BCM15
,
Mou16
,
CH21
]
:

\dfrac{\begin{array}[]{l l}\Delta,i:{\mathbb{I}}\vdash B\,\mathsf{type}\\
\Delta,i:{\mathbb{I}},y:B\vdash C\,\mathsf{type}\\
\Delta,y:B[\epsilon/i]\vdash c_{\epsilon}:C[\epsilon/i]&(\epsilon\in\mathopen{}\left\{0,1\right\}\mathclose{})\\
\Delta,h:\forall({i:{\mathbb{I}}}).B,i:{\mathbb{I}}\vdash c_{\forall}:C[h\,i/b]\\
\Delta,h:\forall({i:{\mathbb{I}}}).B\vdash c_{\forall}[\epsilon/i]=c_{\epsilon}[h\,\epsilon/b]:C[h\,\epsilon/b]&(\epsilon\in\mathopen{}\left\{0,1\right\}\mathclose{})\end{array}\hfill}{\begin{array}[]{l l}\Delta\vdash\Phi\,c_{0}\,c_{1}\,c_{\forall}:\forall\,{i}.(y:B)\to C\\
\mathrm{where}\,\,\Phi\,c_{0}\,c_{1}\,c_{\forall}\,\epsilon\,b=c_{\epsilon}[b/y]&(\epsilon\in\mathopen{}\left\{0,1\right\}\mathclose{})\\
\phantom{\mathrm{where}}\,\,\Delta,i:{\mathbb{I}}\vdash\Phi\,c_{0}\,c_{1}\,c_{\forall}\,{i}\,b=c_{\forall}[\lambda i.b/h]\end{array}\hfill}\text{}

\Phi
-rule in MTraS
(recall
eq.

1
)
:

\dfrac{\begin{array}[]{l l}\hskip-4.30554pt\text{\hypertarget{rule:phi}{{phi}}}\\
{\mathbb{X}},u:{\mathbb{U}}\mathrel{|}\Gamma\vdash C\,\mathsf{type}\\
{\mathbb{X}},u:{\mathbb{U}}\mathrel{|}\Gamma,\_:u\in\partial{\mathbb{U}}\vdash c_{\partial}:C\\
{\mathbb{X}},u:{\mathbb{U}}\mathrel{|}\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\forall\,{u}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}{\between}[{u}]}},\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\Finv[{u}]}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\forall\,{u}}}\vdash c_{\forall}:C[{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathsf{app}_{u}}:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\Finv[{u}]\circ\forall\,{u}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}1}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathsf{reidx}_{u}}:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}1}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}{\between}[{u}]\circ\forall\,{u}}}]\\

\mathrm{where}\,\,{\mathbb{X}},u:{\mathbb{U}}\mathrel{|}\Gamma,\_:u\in\partial{\mathbb{U}}\vdash\Phi_{u}\,c_{\partial}\,c_{\forall}=c_{\partial}:C\\
\phantom{\mathrm{where}}\,\,{\mathbb{X}},u:{\mathbb{U}}\mathrel{|}\Delta,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\Finv[{u}]}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\forall\,{u}}}\vdash\Phi_{u}\,c_{\partial}\,c_{\forall}=c_{\forall}[{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathsf{const}_{u}}:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}1}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\forall\,{u}\circ\Finv[{u}]}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathsf{unmer}_{u}}:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\forall\,{u}\circ{\between}[{u}]}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}1}},\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\Finv[{u}]}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\forall\,{u}}}]:C\end{array}\hfill}\text{\text{}}

Figure 10.
The
\Phi
-rule (sound if
{\mathbb{U}}
is
\top
-slice fully faithful and shard-free).

In
fig.

10
, we
state
BCM’s
\Phi
-rule
[
BCM15
,
Mou16
]
, also known as
\mathsf{extent}

[
CH21
]
; both a slight reformulation adapted to FFTraS and the rule
adapted to MTraS.

In the binary version of the BCM system, or in FFTraS with an interval shape as in cubical type theory, the
\Phi
-combinator allows us to define functions of type
\forall\,{i}.(y:B\,i)\to C\,i\,y
from an action
c_{\epsilon}
at every endpoint
\epsilon
and a compatible action
c_{\forall}
on sections
\forall\,{i}.B\,i
.
When the resulting function
\Phi\,c_{0}\,c_{1}\,c_{\forall}
is applied to an endpoint
\epsilon:{\mathbb{I}}
it just reduces to the corresponding action
\lambda y.c_{\epsilon}
.
When it is applied to an interval variable
i:{\mathbb{I}}
and expression
b
that depends only on
i
and variables fresh for
i
, then the variable
i
can be captured in
b
yielding a section
\lambda i.b:\forall\,{i}.B\,i
to which we can apply the action on sections
c_{\forall}
.

A few remarks are necessary in order to move to MTraS.
First of all, note that the fully applied conclusion of the
\Phi
-rule is
\Delta,i:{\mathbb{I}},y:B\vdash\Phi\,c_{0}\,c_{1}\,c_{\forall}\,{i}\,y:C
.
Of course the endpoints together constitute the boundary of the interval, so if we want to generalize away from cubical type theory, we can expect something like
\Delta,u:{\mathbb{U}},y:B\vdash\Phi\,c_{\partial}\,c_{\forall}\,{u}\,y:C
.
We will assume that the shape
{\mathbb{U}}
is
\top
-slice fully faithful and shard-free.
In order to translate the context
(\Delta,u:{\mathbb{U}},y:B)
to MTraS, recall that in FFTraS (as well as in the BCM system) the variables to the left of
u
are fresh for
u
, whereas those to the right need not be.
In MTraS we put the shape variables in the shape context, so the context translates to
(\Delta,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\Finv[{u}]}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\forall\,{u}}},y:B)
.
In MTraS we will treat this entire thing as a single abstract context
\Gamma
, so we do not grant the domain of the
\Phi
-function any special status; in a way all of
\Gamma
takes the role of
B
.

Then, completely analogously to BCM, we need a type
C
in context
\Gamma
, an object
c_{\partial}:C
whenever
u
is on the boundary, and a compatible action
c_{\forall}
that acts on sections of
\Gamma
and produces sections of
C
, but the quantifier
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\forall\,{u}}
has been brought to the left as
\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\Finv[{u}]}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\forall\,{u}}}
.
Again the resulting term
\Phi_{u}\,c_{\partial}\,c_{\forall}
reduces to
c_{\partial}
when on the boundary, and to
c_{\forall}
when all variables in use are fresh for
u
.
Again, the computation rule for sections is in a non-general context and needs to be forcibly closed under substitution.

Note that the FFTraS/BCM substitutions
h\,i/b
and
h\,\epsilon/b
(i.e.
h\,i/b
where
i
is on the boundary) involving applications, all turn into usages of
{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathsf{app}_{u}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathsf{reidx}_{u}}}
whereas the variable capturing substitution
\lambda i.b/h
has turned into
({\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathsf{const}_{u}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathsf{unmer}_{u}}},\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\Finv[{u}]}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\forall\,{u}}}):(\Delta,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\Finv[{u}]}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\forall\,{u}}})\to(\Delta,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\Finv[{u}]}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\forall\,{u}}},\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\forall\,{u}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}{\between}[{u}]}},\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\Finv[{u}]}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\forall\,{u}}})
which for
\top
-slice fully faithful multipliers is inverse to
(\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\Finv[{u}]}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\forall\,{u}}},{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathsf{app}_{u}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathsf{reidx}_{u}}})
and therefore denotes an inverse of application: variable capture.
Furthermore, regarding the context of
c_{\forall}
, we remark that for
\top
-slice fully faithful multipliers there is an isomorphism of contexts

\displaystyle(\Delta,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\Finv[{u}]}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\forall\,{u}}},y:B,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\forall\,{u}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}{\between}[{u}]}},\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\Finv[{u}]}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\forall\,{u}}})

\displaystyle\cong_{\text{\ref{thm:transpose}}}\mathopen{}\left(\Delta,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\Finv[{u}]}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\forall\,{u}}},\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\forall\,{u}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}{\between}[{u}]}},{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\forall\,{u}}\shortmid y:B\mathopen{}\left[\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\Finv[{u}]}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\forall\,{u}}},{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathsf{app}_{u}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathsf{reidx}_{u}}}\right]\mathclose{},\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\Finv[{u}]}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\forall\,{u}}}\right)\mathclose{}

\displaystyle\cong_{\text{\ref{thm:quantification}}}(\Delta,{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\forall\,{u}}\shortmid y:B,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\Finv[{u}]}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\forall\,{u}}})

so, recalling from
section

7.1.3
that
i:{\mathbb{I}}
translates to
\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\Finv[{i}]}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\forall\,{i}}}
in the internal context, there really is a close correspondence to what happens in the BCM system.

We remark that if
{\mathbb{U}}
is
\top
-slice fully faithful but not necessarily shard-free, then the
\Phi
-rule remains valid for creating terms
of a transpension type

C=\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}{\between}[{u}]}\mathrel{|}D\right\rangle\mathclose{}
.
Indeed, using pole
theorem

9.1
we can then define:

\displaystyle\Phi_{u}\,\mathsf{pole}\,c_{\forall}

\displaystyle:=\mathsf{mod}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}{\between}[{u}]}}\,(\mathsf{unmer}_{u}\cdot_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\forall\,{u}}}c_{\forall}):\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}{\between}[{u}]}\mathrel{|}D\right\rangle\mathclose{}.

The non-trivial computation rule follows from the
\eta
-rule for projections (
proposition

3.3
) and quantification
theorem

6.33
.

Theorem 10.1
.

If
{\mathbb{U}}
is
\top
-slice fully faithful and shard-free, then the
\Phi
-rule (
fig.

10
) is sound and indeed derivable from
for all
C
.

Proof 10.2
.

Use the
\mathsf{case}
-eliminator for
\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}{\between}[{u}]}\mathrel{|}\mathsf{Unit}\right\rangle\mathclose{}
(
theorem

9.4
):

\displaystyle\Phi_{u}\,c_{\partial}\,c_{\forall}

\displaystyle:=\mathsf{case}\,(\mathsf{mod}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}{\between}[{u}]}}\,{\mathsf{unit}})\,\mathsf{of}\mathopen{}\left\{\begin{array}[]{l c l}\mathsf{pole}\mapsto c_{\partial}\mathrel{|}\mathsf{mer}\,\_\mapsto c_{\forall}\end{array}\right\}\mathclose{}.

Figure 11.
Typing rules for the
\Psi
-type.

BCM’s
\Psi
-combinator (
fig.

11
, also known as
\mathsf{Gel}

[
CH21
]
) constructs a line
\forall({i:{\mathbb{I}}}).\mathsf{U}
in the universe with endpoints
A_{\epsilon}
from a relation
R:A_{0}\to A_{1}\to\mathsf{U}
.
A section of the
\Psi
-type with endpoints
a_{\epsilon}
is a proof of
R\,a_{0}\,a_{1}
.
The constructor
{\mathsf{in}\Psi}
creates a section
\forall({i:{\mathbb{I}}}).\Psi_{i}\,A_{0}\,A_{1}\,(x_{0}.x_{1}.R)
from the expected inputs.
The disappearance of
\Theta
in the premises of
\Psi
and
{\mathsf{in}\Psi}
is entirely analogous to the shape application rule
in
fig.

1
.
The eliminator
{\mathsf{out}\Psi}
extracts from a section of the
\Psi
-type the proof that its endpoints satisfy the relation
R
.

Actually using the typing rules of FFTraS and a strictness axiom as in
section

8.3
we can already implement a stronger
\Psi
-type, also given in
fig.

11
, where
\Theta
does not disappear but gets universally quantified.
This is done by strictifying the right hand sides below:
21
21

21

For the identity type, we use pattern-matching abstractions to abbreviate the usage of the J-rule. We are in an extensional type system anyway.

\displaystyle\alpha:\Psi_{i}\,A_{0}\,A_{1}\,(x_{0}.x_{1}.R)

\displaystyle:\cong(\hat{x}_{0}:({\mathsf{refl}}:i\equiv_{{\mathbb{I}}}0)\to A_{0})\times(\hat{x}_{1}:({\mathsf{refl}}:i\equiv_{{\mathbb{I}}}1)\to A_{1})\times{}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad{\between}[{i}]\,(R[({\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}i.\hat{x}_{0})\,0\,{\mathsf{refl}}/x_{0},({\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}i.\hat{x}_{1})\,1\,{\mathsf{refl}}/x_{1}])

\displaystyle{\mathsf{in}\Psi}_{i}\,a_{0}\,a_{1}\,r

\displaystyle:=\alpha^{-1}(\lambda{\mathsf{refl}}.a_{0},\lambda{\mathsf{refl}}.a_{1},\mathsf{mer}[{i}]\,r)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{out}\Psi}(i.q)

\displaystyle:=\mathsf{unmer}(i.\pi_{3}(\alpha(q)))

The fact that this construction is isomorphic to
A_{\epsilon}
at endpoint
\epsilon
follows from our findings about poles in
section

2.2
.

When we move to MTraS, once again we translate the context
(\Delta,u:{\mathbb{U}},\Theta)
to
(\Delta,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\Finv[{u}]}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\forall\,{u}}},\Theta)
, which we treat as a single abstract context
\Gamma
.
By a reasoning identical to that in
section

10.2
, applying
\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\Finv[{u}]}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\forall\,{u}}}
only affects the non-fresh part
\Theta
if the shape
{\mathbb{U}}
is
\top
-slice fully faithful.
This leads to the MTraS rules listed in
fig.

11
.
Note again how substitutions
({\mkern 0.75mu\mathchar 22\mkern-9.75mu\lambda}i.\theta)\,j/\theta
in FFTraS give rise to usages of
{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathsf{app}_{u}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathsf{reidx}_{u}}}
in MTraS.
The usages of
{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathsf{const}_{u}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathsf{unmer}_{u}}}
are entirely absent in FFTraS, but for
\top
-slice fully faithful multipliers, this is an isomorphism anyway.

The eliminator
{\mathsf{out}\Psi}
only eliminates sections.
For
\top
-slice fully faithful and shard-free multipliers, the
\Phi
-rule provides a pattern-matching eliminator which lets us treat the boundary and section cases separately.

Theorem 10.3
.

For
any
multiplier, the
\Psi
-type in
fig.

11
is implementable from the transpension type and the strictness axiom.

Proof 10.4
.

We strictify the right hand sides below:

\displaystyle\alpha:\Psi_{u}\,A\,\mathopen{}\left({\hat{x}}.R\right)\mathclose{}

\displaystyle:\cong(\hat{x}:(\_:u\in\partial U)\to A)\times\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}{\between}[{u}]}\mathrel{|}R\right\rangle\mathclose{},

\displaystyle\mathsf{in}\Psi_{u}\,(\_.a)\,r

\displaystyle:=\alpha^{-1}(\lambda\_.a,\mathsf{mod}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}{\between}[{u}]}}\,r),

\displaystyle{\mathsf{out}\Psi}\cdot_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\forall\,{u}}}q

\displaystyle:=\mathsf{unmer}_{u}\cdot_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\forall\,{u}}}\pi_{2}(\alpha(q)).

The fact that this is isomorphic to
A
on the boundary follows from pole
theorem

9.1

Obviously then the transpension type
\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}{\between}[{u}]}\mathrel{|}T\right\rangle\mathclose{}
is also implementable from the
\Psi
-type as
\Psi_{u}\,{\mathsf{Unit}}\,\mathopen{}\left({\_}.T\right)\mathclose{}
.

10.4.
Transpensivity

The
\Phi
-rule is extremely powerful but not available in all systems.
However, when the codomain
C
is a
\Psi
-type, then the
\mathsf{in}\Psi
-rule is actually quite similar to the
\Phi
-rule if we note that sections of the
\Psi
-type are essentially elements of
R
.
As such, we take an interest in types that are very
\Psi
-like.
We have a monad (idempotent if
{\mathbb{U}}
is
\top
-slice fully faithful)

\displaystyle\bar{\Psi}_{u}A

\displaystyle:=\Psi_{u}\,A\,\mathopen{}\left({{\hat{x}}}.\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\forall\,{u}}\mathrel{|}A{\,\mathsf{ext}}\mathopen{}\left\{u\in\partial{\mathbb{U}}\mathrel{?}{\hat{x}}\,\_\right\}\mathclose{}[{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathsf{app}_{u}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathsf{reidx}_{u}}}]\right\rangle\mathclose{}\right)\mathclose{},

where
A{\,\mathsf{ext}}\mathopen{}\left\{\varphi\mathrel{?}a\right\}\mathclose{}
is the type of elements of
A
that are equal to
a
when
\varphi
holds:

A{\,\mathsf{ext}}\mathopen{}\left\{\varphi\mathrel{?}a\right\}\mathclose{}:=(x:A)\times((\_:\varphi)\to(x\equiv_{A}a)).

Definition 10.5
.

A type is
transpensive over
u
if it is a monad-algebra for
\bar{\Psi}_{u}
.

For
\top
-slice fully faithful and shard-free multipliers,
\Phi
entails that all types are transpensive.
For other systems, the universe of
u
-transpensive types will still be closed at least semantically under many interesting type formers, allowing to eliminate
to
these types in a
\Phi
-like way.

10.5.
Glue
,
Weld
,
mill

\mathsf{Glue}\mathopen{}\left\{A\leftarrow\mathopen{}\left(\varphi\mathrel{?}T\mathrel{;}f\right)\mathclose{}\right\}\mathclose{}
and
\mathsf{Weld}\mathopen{}\left\{A\to\mathopen{}\left(\varphi\mathrel{?}T\mathrel{;}g\right)\mathclose{}\right\}\mathclose{}
are similar to
\mathsf{Strict}
but extend unidirectional functions.
Orton and Pitts
[
OP18
]
already show that
\mathsf{Glue}

[
CCHM17
,
NVD17
]
can be implemented by strictifying a pullback along
A\to(\varphi\to A)

[
ND18b
]
which is definable internally using a
\Sigma
-type.
Dually,
\mathsf{Weld}

[
NVD17
]
can be implemented if there is a type former for pushouts along
\varphi\times A\to A
where
\varphi:\mathsf{Prop}

[
Nuy20
, §6.3.3]
, which is sound in all presheaf categories.

Finally,
\mathsf{mill}

[
ND18b
]
states that
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\forall({u:{\mathbb{U}}})}
preserves
\mathsf{Weld}
and is provable by higher-dimensional pattern matching (where
\circledast
is the applicative operation of the modal type):

\displaystyle\mathsf{mill}:\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\forall\,{u}}\mathrel{|}\mathsf{Weld}\mathopen{}\left\{A\to\mathopen{}\left(\varphi\mathrel{?}T\mathrel{;}g\right)\mathclose{}\right\}\mathclose{}\right\rangle\mathclose{}

\displaystyle\qquad\to\mathsf{Weld}\mathopen{}\left\{\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\forall\,{u}}\mathrel{|}A\right\rangle\mathclose{}\to\mathopen{}\left(\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\forall\,{u}}\mathrel{|}\varphi\right\rangle\mathclose{}\mathrel{?}\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\forall\,{u}}\mathrel{|}T\right\rangle\mathclose{}\mathrel{;}(\mathsf{mod}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\forall\,{u}}}\,g)\circledast{\llcorner\hskip-1.72218pt\lrcorner}\right)\mathclose{}\right\}\mathclose{}

\displaystyle\mathsf{mill}\,\hat{w}=\mathsf{unmer}_{u}\cdot_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\forall\,{u}}}{}

\displaystyle\qquad{\mathsf{case}\,(\mathsf{app}_{u}\cdot_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Finv[{u}]}}(\hat{w}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathsf{drop}_{u}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathsf{const}_{u}}}))\,\mathsf{of}\mathopen{}\left\{\begin{array}[]{l c l}\mathsf{weld}\,a\mapsto\mathsf{mod}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}{\between}[{u}]}}\,(\mathsf{weld}\,(\mathsf{mod}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\forall\,{u}}}\,(a^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathsf{app}_{u}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathsf{reidx}_{u}}})))\\
\varphi\mathrel{?}t\mapsto\mathsf{mod}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}{\between}[{u}]}}\,(\mathsf{mod}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\forall\,{u}}}\,(t^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathsf{app}_{u}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathsf{reidx}_{u}}}))\end{array}\right\}\mathclose{}}.

In the first clause, we get an element
a:A
and can proceed as in
section

7.3
.
In the second clause, we are asserted that
\varphi
holds (call the witness
p
) so that the left hand
\mathsf{Weld}
-type equals
T
, and we are given
t:T
.
Then inside the meridian constructor
\mathsf{mod}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}{\between}[{u}]}}\,
we know that
\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\forall\,{u}}\mathrel{|}\varphi\right\rangle\mathclose{}
holds as this is proven by
\mathsf{mod}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\forall\,{u}}}\,(p^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathsf{app}_{u}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathsf{reidx}_{u}}})
; hence the
\mathsf{Weld}
-type in the codomain simplifies to
\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\forall\,{u}}\mathrel{|}T\right\rangle\mathclose{}
.
When
\varphi
holds and
t=\mathsf{weld}\,a=g\,a
, then the
\mathsf{weld}
-constructor of the right hand
\mathsf{Weld}
-type reduces to
(\mathsf{mod}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\forall\,{u}}}\,g)\circledast{\llcorner\hskip-1.72218pt\lrcorner}
which effectively applies
g
under the
\mathsf{mod}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\forall\,{u}}}\,
-constructor, so that both clauses match as required.

10.6.
Locally fresh names

Nominal type theory is modelled in the Schanuel topos
[
Pit14
]
which is a subcategory of nullary affine cubical sets
\mathrm{Psh}({}^{0}{{\mathsf{Cube}}_{\Box}})
(
example

6.16
).
As fibrancy is not considered in this paper, we will work directly in
\mathrm{Psh}({}^{0}{{\mathsf{Cube}}_{\Box}})
.
Names can be modelled using the multiplier
{\llcorner\hskip-1.72218pt\lrcorner}*(i:{\mathbb{I}})
.
Interestingly, the fresh weakening functor
\Finv_{(i:{\mathbb{I}})}
is then
inverse
to its left adjoint
\exists_{(i:{\mathbb{I}})}
.
By consequence, we get
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\exists\,{i}}\cong{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\forall\,{i}}=:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\text{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathsf{N}$}}}\,i}
(the fresh name quantifier) with inverse
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Finv[{i}]}\cong{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}{\between}[{i}]}
.
For consistency, we will only use
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\text{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathsf{N}$}}}\,i}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Finv[{i}]}
, and these will be each other’s left names.
The relevant 2-cells are
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathsf{app}_{i}}:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Finv[{i}]\circ\text{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathsf{N}$}}}\,i}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}1}
and its inverse
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathsf{copy}_{i}}:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}1}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Finv[{i}]\circ\text{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathsf{N}$}}}\,i}
(these are each other’s left names), as well as
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathsf{const}_{i}}:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}1}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\text{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathsf{N}$}}}\,i\circ\Finv[{i}]}
and its inverse
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathsf{drop}_{i}}:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\text{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathsf{N}$}}}\,i\circ\Finv[{i}]}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}1}
(these are each other’s left names).

The nominal dependent type system FreshMLTT
[
PMD15
]
used in Pitts’s examples of interest
[
Pit14
]
is substantially different from ours:

•

It features a name swapping operation that is semantically
not
merely a substitution.

•

Freshness for a name
i
is not a modality or a type, but a judgement that can be derived for an expression
t
if and only if
t
is invariant under swapping
i
with a newly introduced name
j
. As a consequence, freshness propagates through type and term constructors.

•

Many equalities are strict where we can only guarantee an isomorphism.

For these reasons, we do not try to formally state that we can support locally fresh names in the sense of FreshMLTT.
Nevertheless, in
fig.

12
we give at least a heuristic
\mathopen{}\left\llbracket{\llcorner\hskip-1.72218pt\lrcorner}\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}
for translating programs in a subsystem of FreshMLTT to programs in the current system.
This subsystem does not feature name swapping, but it does feature the
non-binding abstractions
originally defined in terms of it, as well as locally fresh names.

name

FreshMLTT

MTraS

name quantification

\text{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathsf{N}$}}}[i:N].T

\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\text{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathsf{N}$}}}({i:{\mathbb{I}}})}\mathrel{|}\mathopen{}\left\llbracket T\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}\right\rangle\mathclose{}

name abstraction

\alpha[i:N].t

\mathsf{mod}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\text{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathsf{N}$}}}({i:{\mathbb{I}}})}}\,\mathopen{}\left\llbracket t\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}

name application

t@i

\mathsf{app}_{i}\cdot_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Finv[{i}]}}\mathopen{}\left\llbracket t\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}

non-binding quant.

\mathopen{}\left\langle\mathopen{}\left\langle i:N\right\rangle\mathclose{}\right\rangle\mathclose{}.T

\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Finv[{i}]}\mathrel{|}\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\text{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathsf{N}$}}}\,i}\mathrel{|}\mathopen{}\left\llbracket T\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}[{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathsf{app}_{i}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathsf{copy}_{i}}}]\right\rangle\mathclose{}\right\rangle\mathclose{}

non-binding abs.

\mathopen{}\left\langle i:N\right\rangle\mathclose{}.t

\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{copy}_{i}\,\mathopen{}\left\llbracket t\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}:=\\
\mathsf{mod}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Finv[{i}]}}\,(\mathsf{mod}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\text{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathsf{N}$}}}\,i}}\,(\mathopen{}\left\llbracket t\right\rrbracket\mathclose{}[{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathsf{app}_{i}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathsf{copy}_{i}}}]))\end{array}

locally fresh name

\nu[i:N].t

\mathsf{drop}_{i}\cdot_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\text{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathsf{N}$}}}\,i}}(\mathsf{mod}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Finv[{i}]}}\,\mathopen{}\left\llbracket t\right\rrbracket\mathclose{})

Figure 12.
A heuristic for translating FreshMLTT
[
PMD15
]
to the current system.

Ordinary name quantification is simply translated to the modality
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\text{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathsf{N}$}}}\,i}
, and as usual application corresponds to the modal projection function (
proposition

3.3
).
The non-binding abstraction in FreshMLTT abstracts over a name that is already in scope,
without
shadowing, i.e. it is a variable capturing operation.
This is translated essentially to the 2-cell
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathsf{copy}_{i}}:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}1}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Finv[{i}]\circ\text{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathsf{N}$}}}\,i}
, which is inverse to
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathsf{app}_{i}}
as we have seen earlier for a variable capturing operation (
section

10.2
).
Finally, a locally fresh name abstraction
\nu[i:N].t
brings a name
i
into scope in its body
t
, but requires that
t
be fresh for
i
; in our system we would say that
t
is a subterm of modality
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\text{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathsf{N}$}}}\,i\circ\Finv[{i}]}
.
The type of
\nu[i:N].t
is the same as the type of
t
, which we can justify with the isomorphism
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathsf{drop}_{i}}:{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\text{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathsf{N}$}}}\,i\circ\Finv[{i}]}\cong{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}1}
.
This isomorphism is essentially the content of the modal projection function of
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Finv[{i}]}
, which we use to translate locally fresh name abstractions.

Note that for general multipliers,
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Finv[{i}]}
does not have an internal left adjoint and hence not a modal projection function either.
For the nullary affine interval, however,
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Finv[{i}]}\cong{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}{\between}[{i}]}
, so the projection function is essentially
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathsf{unmer}_{i}}
!

Example 10.6
.

Consider Pitts’s implementation of higher dimensional pattern matching
[
Pit14
]
:

\displaystyle j:(\text{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathsf{N}$}}}[i:N].A\uplus B)\to(\text{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathsf{N}$}}}[i:N].A)\uplus(\text{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathsf{N}$}}}[i:N].B)

\displaystyle j\,\hat{c}=\nu[i:N].\mathsf{case}\,\hat{c}@i\,\mathsf{of}\mathopen{}\left\{\begin{array}[]{l c l}\mathsf{inl}\,a&\mapsto&\mathsf{inl}\,(\mathopen{}\left\langle i:N\right\rangle\mathclose{}.a)\\
\mathsf{inr}\,b&\mapsto&\mathsf{inr}\,(\mathopen{}\left\langle i:N\right\rangle\mathclose{}.b)\end{array}\right\}\mathclose{}

A brainless translation using
fig.

12
yields a type mismatch, because the non-binding abstractions will put the freshness constructor inside the coproduct constructors, whereas the translation of the locally fresh name abstraction mentions it again on the outside.
This is related to our earlier remark that in FreshMLTT, freshness silently propagates through type and term constructors, so here we have to manually intervene.
This is also necessary to insert invertible 2-cell substitutions in some places,
and to check whether we need a modal argument (i.e. the modality is handled at the judgemental level) or we need to explicitly use the modal constructor as is always done in
fig.

12
.
Doing so, we find

\displaystyle j:\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\text{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathsf{N}$}}}\,i}\mathrel{|}A\uplus B\right\rangle\mathclose{}\to\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\text{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathsf{N}$}}}\,i}\mathrel{|}A\right\rangle\mathclose{}\uplus\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\text{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathsf{N}$}}}\,i}\mathrel{|}B\right\rangle\mathclose{}

\displaystyle j\,\hat{c}=\mathsf{drop}_{i}\cdot_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\text{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathsf{N}$}}}\,i}}\mathsf{case}\,(\mathsf{app}_{i}\cdot_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Finv[{i}]}}(\hat{c}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathsf{drop}_{i}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathsf{const}_{i}}}))\,\mathsf{of}\mathopen{}\left\{\begin{array}[]{l c l}\mathsf{inl}\,a\mapsto\mathsf{mod}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Finv[{i}]}}\,(\mathsf{inl}\,(\mathsf{mod}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\text{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathsf{N}$}}}\,i}}\,(a^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathsf{app}_{i}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathsf{copy}_{i}}})))\\
\mathsf{inr}\,b\mapsto\mathsf{mod}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Finv[{i}]}}\,(\mathsf{inr}\,(\mathsf{mod}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\text{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathsf{N}$}}}\,i}}\,(b^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathsf{app}_{i}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathsf{copy}_{i}}})))\end{array}\right\}\mathclose{},

which is
exactly
what we found in
section

7.3
adapted to our convention that we only want to mention
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Finv[{i}]}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\forall\,{i}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathsf{app}_{i}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathsf{copy}_{i}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathsf{const}_{i}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathsf{drop}_{i}}
.

Example 10.7
.

Consider Pitts et al.’s
implementation

[
PMD15
, ex. 2.2]
of what is essentially BCM’s
\Phi
-rule
[
Mou16
,
BCM15
]
since the boundary is empty:

\displaystyle g:((\text{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathsf{N}$}}}[i:N].A)\to\text{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathsf{N}$}}}[i:N].B)\to\text{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathsf{N}$}}}[i:N].(A\to B)

\displaystyle g\,f=\alpha[i:N].(\lambda x.f\,(\mathopen{}\left\langle i:N\right\rangle\mathclose{}.x)\,@\,i.

We can translate this to the current system using
fig.

12
:

\displaystyle g:(\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\text{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathsf{N}$}}}\,i}\mathrel{|}A\right\rangle\mathclose{}\to\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\text{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathsf{N}$}}}\,i}\mathrel{|}B\right\rangle\mathclose{})\to\mathopen{}\left\langle{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\text{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathsf{N}$}}}\,i}\mathrel{|}A\to B\right\rangle\mathclose{}

\displaystyle g\,f=\mathsf{mod}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\text{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathsf{N}$}}}\,i}}\,\mathopen{}\left(\lambda x.\mathsf{app}_{i}\cdot_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Finv[{i}]}}(f[{\scalebox{0.85}{\text{\faIconFromMacro{faKey}}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathsf{drop}_{i}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathsf{const}_{i}}}]\,(\mathsf{mod}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\text{\reflectbox{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\mathsf{N}$}}}\,i}}\,(x^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathsf{app}_{i}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\mathsf{copy}_{i}}})))\right)\mathclose{}.

The effect of conflating
\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\exists\,{i}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Finv[{i}]}}
with
\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\forall\,{i}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}{\between}[{i}]}}
is that affine function application no longer renders the non-fresh part of the context inaccessible using
\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\exists\,{i}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\Finv[{i}]}}
but instead universally quantifies it using
\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\forall\,{i}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}{\between}[{i}]}}
, so that we can capture variables as in FreshMLTT.
Remarkably, we do
not
need the
\Phi
-rule (
fig.

10
) for this nor pattern-matching for the transpension type (
fig.

8
), although these rules hold as the affine cubical interval is
\top
-slice fully faithful and shard-free (
example

6.16
).

11.
Conclusion

To summarize, the transpension type can be defined in a broad class of presheaf models and generalizes previous internalization operators.
For now, we only present an extensional type system without an algorithmic typing judgement.
The major hurdles towards producing an intensional version with decidable type-checking, are the following:

•

We need to decide equality of 2-cells. Solutions may exist in the literature on higher-dimensional rewriting. Alternatively, we need to extend MTT with a language to reason about 2-cell equality.

•

The substitution modality should ideally reduce like ordinary substitution.
Remark

5.5
explores what is needed for this to work.

•

We need a syntax-directed way to close the section computation rules of
\Phi
(
fig.

10
) and transpension elimination (
section

9
) under substitution.

•

We need to decide whether the boundary predicate, or any similar predicate about shape variables such as
i\equiv_{{\mathbb{I}}}0
in cubical type theory, is true.
This problem has been dealt with in special cases, e.g. in implementations of cubical type theory
[
VMA19
]
.

Applications include all applications (discussed in
section

1
) of the presheaf internalization operators recovered from the transpension type in
section

10
.
Moreover, our modal approach to shape variables via multipliers allows the inclusion of Pinyo and Kraus’s twisted prism functor
[
PK20
]
as a semantics of an interval variable, which we believe is an important advancement towards higher-dimensional directed type theory.

Appendix A
Changelog

The first preprint of this paper appeared in 2020 and is subsumed in
[
Nuy20
, ch. 7]
.
Since then, there have been significant changes, primarily terminological ones.
To help out readers coming back to this paper after having consulted earlier versions (or associated presentations), we list the most important changes here.

A.1.
Terminology

A.1.1.
Definition

6.2

•

Copointed
multipliers were formerly called
semicartesian
,

•

Multipliers that are
comonads
were formerly called
3/4-cartesian
,

•

\top
-slice faithful
multipliers were formerly called
cancellative
,

•

\top
-slice full
multipliers were formerly called
affine
,

•

\top
-slice shard-free
multipliers were formerly called
connection-free
, and
shards
were formerly called
connections
,

•

\top
-slice right adjoint
multipliers were formerly called
quantifiable
.

A.1.2.
Definition

6.5

•

Unpointable
objects were formerly called
spooky
,

•

Not objectwise pointable
categories were formerly called
spooky
.

A.1.3.
Definition

6.23

Presheafwise
faithful/full/shard-free/right adjoint multipliers were formerly called
providently
cancellative/affine/connection-free/quantifiable.

A previous version of the paper featured a different definition of shards/connections and presheafwise shard-freedom / provident connection-freedom, that was based on the
indirect
boundary and
indirectly
dimensionally split morphisms (
section

A.1.4
).
These notions are obsolete and are retained in the technical report
[
Nuy23b
]
under the names
indirect shard
and
indirect shard-freedom
(alongside the direct notions from
definition

6.23
) solely for consistency with
[
Nuy20
, ch. 7]
.

A.1.4.
Definition

6.24

A previous version of this paper had a different definition of boundary and of dimensionally split cells which was defined using the pullback of
\Xi\ltimes\mathbf{y}U\to\mathbf{y}U\supseteq\partial U
.
These notions are obsolete and are retained in the technical report
[
Nuy23b
]
under the name
indirect boundary
and
indirectly dimensionally split
cells (alongside the direct notions) solely for consistency with
[
Nuy20
, ch. 7]
.

As the notions coincide for
\Xi=\top
,
theorem

9.3
holds w.r.t. the indirect boundary if
\Xi=\top
.
By construction, the indirect boundary is respected by substitution, while the transpension type is generally not if the multiplier is not
\top
-slice fully faithful (
section

2.1.7
).
Hence, the indirect notions generally diverge from the direct ones and the indirect version of
theorem

9.3
breaks down if
\Xi\neq\top
and the multiplier is not
\top
-slice fully faithful.

A.1.5.
Theorem

6.30

The
quotient theorem
was formerly called
kernel theorem
.

A.2.
Ticks

A previous version of this paper assigned names to locks, called
ticks
, after the tick of the (c)lock in
[
BGM17
]
.
The usage of this notation is orthogonal to the introduction of MTraS; a version of the original MTT paper
[
GKNB20b
]
in tick notation is included in
[
Nuy20
, §5.3]
.
An improved notation is proposed in
[
Nuy23a
]
.

A.3.
Lockless notation

A previous version of this paper supplemented the MTT notation with a so-called
lockless
notation in which
(\Gamma,\text{\faIconFromMacro{faLock}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\kappa}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.3,0}\mu}})
was denoted as
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0}\kappa}(\Gamma)
.
This notation was abolished in favour of
left adjoint reminders
(
section

3.4
).
